<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> For arbitrary complex inputs in $`L^3(\mathbb R^2)`$, we prove the pointwise maximal $`L^3\times L^3\to L^{3/2}`$ estimate for the triangular Hilbert transform, with the supremum over both hard truncation endpoints. The estimate yields joint almost-everywhere and <i>L</i><sup>3/2</sup> convergence as the lower endpoint tends to zero and the upper endpoint tends to infinity. This also proves the conjectured scalar estimate at the symmetric point.

- **C1** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): There is an absolute constant $C$ such that, for every pair of complex functions $F,G\in L^3(\mathbb R^2)$, $$\\|B_*(F,G)\\|_{L^{3/2}(\mathbb R^2)} \le C\\|F\\|_{L^3(\mathbb R^2)}\\|G\\|_{L^3(\mathbb R^2)}.$$ The supremum is over both finite hard truncation endpoints.
  - Explanation: The maximal estimate controls both hard truncation endpoints inside the spatial norm.
- **C2** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-hessian)): On the cone of matrices positive definite on a fixed support, $\Phi$ is smooth. For Hermitian directions $K,L$ on that support, $$d\Phi_P(K)=\frac32\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(\sqrt P K), \qquad d^2\Phi_P(K,L)=\mathcal B_P(K,L).$$ If $p_i>0$ are the eigenvalues on the support, then in an orthonormal eigenbasis $$\label{eq:hessian-metric} b_P(K)=\frac32\sum_{i,j} \frac{\|K_{ij}\|^2}{\sqrt{p_i}+\sqrt{p_j}}.$$ In particular, $\mathcal B_P$ is a positive definite symmetric bilinear form on the real space of supported Hermitian directions, and $$\|\mathcal B_P(K,L)\|\le b_P(K)^{1/2}b_P(L)^{1/2}.$$
  - Explanation: The energy has a supported Hessian with positivity and a Cauchy–Schwarz bound.
- **C3** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-order)): Let $P,Q$ be positive semidefinite matrices on the same Hilbert space, let $a\ge1$, and suppose $P\le aQ$. Every Hermitian $K$ supported on $P$ is supported on $Q$, and $$\label{eq:matrix-order} b_P(K)\ge a^{-1/2}b_Q(K).$$
  - Explanation: Matrix order compares supported Hessian costs in the reverse direction.
- **C4** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-insertion)): Let $R:E\to F$ be a linear map between finite-dimensional complex Hilbert spaces, and let $L:F\to F$ be Hermitian. The matrix $R^*LR$ is supported on $R^*R$, and $$\label{eq:insertion-bound} b_{R^*R}(R^*LR) \le\frac34\lVert L\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}^2\Phi(R^*R).$$
  - Explanation: The inserted Gram cost is controlled by the energy and the insertion norm.
- **C5** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-mixed-trace)): Let $W_v:H_{v+1}\to H_v$ be linear maps between finite-dimensional complex Hilbert spaces, with row stars $R_v$ and Gram matrices $T_v$ as in <a href="#eq:row-star" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:row-star">[eq:row-star]</a>–<a href="#eq:star-grams" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:star-grams">[eq:star-grams]</a>. For Hermitian maps $D_v:H_v\to H_v$, put $D_* =\max_v\lVert D_v\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}$. Then $$\label{eq:mixed-trace} \left\|\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(D_0W_0D_1W_1D_2W_2)\right\| \le \frac53D_*\sum_{v=0}^2 b_{T_v}(R_v^*D_vR_v).$$ The constant is independent of the dimensions of the spaces.
  - Explanation: The cyclic trace is bounded by star costs independently of the dimensions.
- **C6** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-star-smoothness)): For fixed finite edge arrays, each $e_v$ is smooth in all centers and positive variances. If only the parameters at vertex $v$ vary, the support of $T_v$ is fixed. If the parameters at either or both other vertices vary while those at $v$ are fixed, the support of $S_v$ is fixed.
  - Explanation: Positive Gaussian weights give smooth star energies with the stated fixed supports.
- **C7** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-heat-identities)): Fix a positive vector $\boldsymbol\alpha$ and set $\sigma_i=\alpha_i s$. For all centers and all $s>0$, $$\begin{aligned} 2\partial_s e_v &=\sum_i\alpha_i\partial_{p_i}^2e_v -\alpha_vV_v-\sum_{w\ne v}\alpha_wY_{v,w}, \label{eq:heat-identity}\\ \partial_{p_{v+1}}\partial_{p_{v-1}}e_v&=Z_v. \label{eq:heat-mixed} \end{aligned}$$
  - Explanation: Heat differentiation separates center derivatives from score costs.
- **C8** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-neighbors)): For every choice of the weighted matrices and every vertex $w$, $$\label{eq:heat-neighbors} Y_{w+1,w}+Y_{w-1,w}\le\sqrt2\,V_w.$$
  - Explanation: The neighboring column costs are controlled by the row score cost.
- **C9** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-plane-dissipation)): Fix finite edge arrays and a positive vector $\boldsymbol\alpha$, and evaluate the energies at $\sigma_i=\alpha_i s$. Set $$J_v(d,s)=\int_{\Pi_d}e_v\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\pi_d, \qquad J(d,s)=\sum_vJ_v(d,s), \qquad \Sigma_\alpha=\sum_i\alpha_i.$$ These integrals are finite, continuously differentiable in $s>0$, and twice continuously differentiable in $d$. Moreover, $$\label{eq:heat-plane-dissipation} 2\partial_sJ(d,s)\le\int_{\Pi_d}\sum_w \left[-\alpha_w+ \sqrt2\max\{0,\Sigma_\alpha/2-\alpha_w\}\right]V_w\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\pi_d.$$ In particular, for each permutation of $(1,\lambda,\lambda)$, $$\label{eq:heat-strict} -\partial_sJ(d,s)\ge c_*\int_{\Pi_d}\sum_wV_w\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\pi_d, \qquad c_* = \frac12\min\{1-\tfrac35\sqrt2,\tfrac{11}{10}-\tfrac12\sqrt2\}>0.$$
  - Explanation: Plane integration yields strict dissipation for the specified variance rates.
- **C10** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-initial-energy)): Let $h>0$, let $N\ge0$ be an integer, and let $A_v(i,j)$ be the finite complex arrays above. For any $d\in\mathbb R$ and variances $\sigma_v\ge h^2$, $$\label{eq:initial-energy} \int_{\Pi_d}\sum_ve_v\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\pi_d \le C_0\mathcal S(A),\qquad C_0=2\sqrt2\left(1+\sqrt{2/\pi}\right).$$ The same bound holds if any entries are replaced by smaller entries in absolute value before forming the weighted matrices.
  - Explanation: Variances no smaller than the mesh scale give a uniform initial energy bound.
- **C11** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-line-maximal)): On either of these measure spaces, write $M$ for the corresponding centered maximal operator. For $f\in L^1$ and $\rho>0$, $$\mu\{Mf>\rho\}\le\frac3\rho\\|f\\|_1.$$ For every $1<q<\infty$ and $f\in L^q$, $$\label{eq:line-maximal-strong} \\|Mf\\|_q^q\le \frac{6q\,2^{q-1}}{q-1}\\|f\\|_q^q.$$ Moreover, there is an absolute constant $C_g$ such that, whenever $h>0$, $\sigma\ge h^2$ and $n\in\mathbb Z$, $$\label{eq:discrete-gaussian-maximal} \sum_{m\in\mathbb Z}h g_\sigma((m-n)h)\|f(m)\| \le C_g M_{\mathbb Z}f(n).$$
  - Explanation: Centered maximal estimates control continuous and discrete line averages.
- **C12** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-rank-change)): For finite rectangular complex matrices $R_-,R_+$ of the same size, put $R(z)=(1-z)R_-+zR_+$ and $D=R_+-R_-$. Then $$\label{eq:rank-change-derivative} \begin{aligned} &\Phi(R_+^*R_+)-\Phi(R_-^*R_-)\\ &\quad=3\int_0^1\operatorname{Re} \langle R(z)\sqrt{R(z)^*R(z)},D\rangle_{\mathrm{HS}}\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm dz. \end{aligned}$$ For every rectangular matrix $R$ and every entry $(i,j)$, $$\label{eq:entry-gradient} \|(R\sqrt{R^*R})_{ij}\| \le\\|\operatorname{row}_iR\\|_2\, \\|\operatorname{col}_jR\\|_2.$$
  - Explanation: The energy variation formula remains valid through rank changes.
- **C13** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-masked-dissipation)): For every $h>0$, $N\ge0$, complex arrays $A_v$ on $\{-N,\ldots,N\}^2$, and endpoint choices as above, let $\alpha$ be any permutation of $(1,11/10,11/10)$ and use variances $\sigma_v=\alpha_vs$. The corresponding masked costs satisfy $$\label{eq:masked-dissipation} \int_{s_-}^{s_+}\int_{\Pi_0} \sum_{v=0}^2 V_v(p,s)\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\pi\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm ds \le C\mathcal S(A).$$ The constant is independent of the arrays, the mesh, the endpoints, and the number of distinct endpoint values.
  - Explanation: Independent entry endpoints preserve a uniform bound on accumulated dissipation.
- **C14** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-selected-kernel)): Let $h>0$, let $N\ge0$ be an integer, set $u_i=ih$, and let $A_v(i,j)$, $v=0,1,2$, be complex arrays indexed by $-N\le i,j\le N$. Assign an arbitrary endpoint pair $h\le\varepsilon_{ij}<\rho_{ij}<\infty$ to each $(i,j)$. Then $$\label{eq:selected-kernel} \left\|h^3\sum_{i,j,l}A_0(i,j)A_1(j,l)A_2(l,i) I_{\varepsilon_{ij},\rho_{ij}}(u_i+u_j+u_l)\right\| \le C\prod_{v=0}^2\left(\sum_{i,j}h^2\|A_v(i,j)\|^3\right)^{1/3}.$$ The constant is independent of the arrays, mesh and selected endpoints.
  - Explanation: The finite mesh estimate allows independently selected smooth endpoints.
- **C15** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-smooth-kernel-maximal)): For all complex $F,G\in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R^2)$, $$\left\\|\sup_{0<\varepsilon<R} \|B^I_{\varepsilon,R}(F,G)\|\right\\|_{L^{3/2}(\mathbb R^2)} \le C\\|F\\|_{L^3(\mathbb R^2)}\\|G\\|_{L^3(\mathbb R^2)}.$$ The supremum is measurable and the constant is absolute.
  - Explanation: Finite maxima and mesh limits give the smooth kernel maximal estimate.
- **C16** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-maximal-hard-kernel)): There are an absolute constant $C$ and functions $E_r$, $r>0$, such that, for $t\ne0$, apart from endpoint values, $$\label{eq:maximal-hard-decomposition} I_{\varepsilon,R}(t) =\frac{c_0}{t}\mathbf1_{\{\varepsilon<\|t\|<R\}} +E_\varepsilon(t)-E_R(t), \qquad c_0=-\frac{2}{3\sqrt{6\pi}},$$ and $$\label{eq:maximal-error-envelope} \|E_r(t)\|\le\frac{C}{r}\left(1+\frac{\|t\|}{r}\right)^{-2} \qquad(t\ne0).$$
  - Explanation: The smooth kernel splits into a hard singular kernel and endpoint errors.
- **C17** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-pointwise-endpoint-error)): For complex $F,G\in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R^2)$ and every $r>0$, $$\left\|\int_\mathbb RF(x+t,y)G(x,y+t)E_r(t)\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm dt\right\| \le C\bigl[M_x(\|F\|^2)(x,y)M_y(\|G\|^2)(x,y)\bigr]^{1/2}.$$ The right side has $L^{3/2}(\mathbb R^2)$ norm at most $C\\|F\\|_3\\|G\\|_3$.
  - Explanation: The endpoint errors share a pointwise majorant from coordinate maximal functions.
- **C18** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-compact-maximal)): For all complex $F,G\in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R^2)$, $$\\|B_*(F,G)\\|_{L^{3/2}(\mathbb R^2)} \le C\\|F\\|_{L^3(\mathbb R^2)}\\|G\\|_{L^3(\mathbb R^2)},$$ with an absolute constant. The supremum defining $B_*$ is over both finite hard truncation endpoints, inside the spatial norm.
  - Explanation: Compact smooth inputs satisfy the hard maximal estimate.
- **C19** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-finite-truncations)): For complex $F,G\in L^3(\mathbb R^2)$, there is a set of full measure on which every finite truncation is absolutely convergent and depends continuously on $(\varepsilon,R)$ in $0<\varepsilon<R<\infty$. The resulting family is independent of the representatives of $F,G$ up to a common null set. Moreover, $$\label{eq:elementary-bilinear} \left\\|\int_{\varepsilon<\|t\|<R} \|F(x+t,y)G(x,y+t)\|\,\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm dt}{\|t\|}\right\\|_p \le 2\log(R/\varepsilon)\\|F\\|_3\\|G\\|_3.$$
  - Explanation: All finite truncations coexist outside a common null set and vary continuously with endpoints.
- **C20** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-bilinear-pv)): For all complex $F,G\in L^3(\mathbb R^2)$, the joint limit $$B(F,G)=\lim_{\substack{\varepsilon\downarrow0\\R\uparrow\infty}} B_{\varepsilon,R}(F,G)$$ exists almost everywhere and in $L^{3/2}(\mathbb R^2)$, with $\\|B(F,G)\\|_{3/2}\le C_M\\|F\\|_3\\|G\\|_3$. More precisely, $$\label{eq:maximal-tail-convergence} \left\\|\sup_{\substack{0<\varepsilon<1/n\\R>n}} \|B_{\varepsilon,R}(F,G)-B(F,G)\|\right\\|_{3/2} \longrightarrow0.$$ The limit is the unique bounded bilinear extension of the principal-value operator on compactly supported smooth inputs.
  - Explanation: The joint principal value exists almost everywhere and in the output norm.
- **C21** (bound, [source](#stmt-cor-scalar-pv)): For complex $F_0,F_1,F_2\in L^3(\mathbb R^2)$, every finite flat truncation is absolutely integrable, and $$\sup_{0<\varepsilon<R<\infty} \|\mathcal L_{\varepsilon,R}(F_0,F_1,F_2)\| \le C_M\prod_{v=0}^2\\|F_v\\|_3.$$ Its joint scalar principal value exists and equals $\int F_0 B(F_1,F_2)$. This limiting form is the unique bounded trilinear extension of its compact smooth definition.
  - Explanation: Pairing gives the flat scalar bound and its joint principal value.
- **C22** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-prop-flat-equivalence)): For all complex $G_0,G_1,G_2\in L^3(\mathbb R^2)$, every finite simplex truncation is absolutely integrable, and $$\sup_{0<\varepsilon<R<\infty} \|\Lambda_{\varepsilon,R}(G_0,G_1,G_2)\| \le C_M\prod_{v=0}^2\\|G_v\\|_3.$$ Its joint scalar principal value exists for every such triple and is the unique bounded trilinear extension of its compact smooth definition. The optimal constants in the uniform flat and simplex scalar estimates are equal.
  - Explanation: Norm preserving coordinate maps equate the optimal flat and simplex scalar constants.

      "exact_statement": "Fix finite edge arrays and a positive vector $\\boldsymbol\\alpha$, and evaluate the energies at $\\sigma_i=\\alpha_i s$. Moreover, $$\\label{eq:heat-plane-dissipation}\n 2\\partial_sJ(d,s)\\le\\int_{\\Pi_d}\\sum_w\n \\left[-\\alpha_w+\n \\sqrt2\\max\\{0,\\Sigma_\\alpha/2-\\alpha_w\\}\\right]V_w\\,\\mathop{}\\!\\mathrm d\\pi_d.$$ In particular, for each permutation of $(1,\\lambda,\\lambda)$, $$\\label{eq:heat-strict}\n -\\partial_sJ(d,s)\\ge c_*\\int_{\\Pi_d}\\sum_wV_w\\,\\mathop{}\\!\\mathrm d\\pi_d,\n \\qquad\n c_* = \\frac12\\min\\{1-\\tfrac35\\sqrt2,\\tfrac{11}{10}-\\tfrac12\\sqrt2\\}>0.$$",

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-line-maximal",
        "exact_quote": "On either of these measure spaces, write $M$ for the corresponding centered maximal operator. For $f\\in L^1$ and $\\rho>0$, $$\\mu\\{Mf>\\rho\\}\\le\\frac3\\rho\\|f\\|_1.$$ For every $1<q<\\infty$ and $f\\in L^q$, $$\\label{eq:line-maximal-strong}\n \\|Mf\\|_q^q\\le\n \\frac{6q\\,2^{q-1}}{q-1}\\|f\\|_q^q.$$ Moreover, there is an absolute constant $C_g$ such that, whenever $h>0$, $\\sigma\\ge h^2$ and $n\\in\\mathbb Z$, $$\\label{eq:discrete-gaussian-maximal}\n \\sum_{m\\in\\mathbb Z}h g_\\sigma((m-n)h)|f(m)|\n \\le C_g M_{\\mathbb Z}f(n).$$",
        "source_id": "PM-082.3"
      },

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-rank-change",
        "exact_quote": "For finite rectangular complex matrices $R_-,R_+$ of the same size, put $R(z)=(1-z)R_-+zR_+$ and $D=R_+-R_-$. Then $$\\label{eq:rank-change-derivative}\n \\begin{aligned}\n &\\Phi(R_+^*R_+)-\\Phi(R_-^*R_-)\\\\\n &\\quad=3\\int_0^1\\operatorname{Re}\n   \\langle R(z)\\sqrt{R(z)^*R(z)},D\\rangle_{\\mathrm{HS}}\\,\\mathop{}\\!\\mathrm dz.\n \\end{aligned}$$ For every rectangular matrix $R$ and every entry $(i,j)$, $$\\label{eq:entry-gradient}\n |(R\\sqrt{R^*R})_{ij}|\n \\le\\|\\operatorname{row}_iR\\|_2\\,\n       \\|\\operatorname{col}_jR\\|_2.$$",
        "source_id": "PM-082.3"
      },
      "evidence": [

          "anchor": "stmt-lem-rank-change",

          "source_id": "PM-082.3"

        "anchor": "stmt-prop-selected-kernel",
        "exact_quote": "Let $h>0$, let $N\\ge0$ be an integer, set $u_i=ih$, and let $A_v(i,j)$, $v=0,1,2$, be complex arrays indexed by $-N\\le i,j\\le N$. <a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **Hard truncated and maximal bilinear operators** ([source](#sec:introduction)): For complex functions $F,G$ on $\mathbb R^2$ and $0<\varepsilon<R<\infty$, write $$B_{\varepsilon,R}(F,G)(x,y) =\int_{\varepsilon<\|t\|<R}F(x+t,y)G(x,y+t)\,\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm dt}{t}, \qquad B_*(F,G)=\sup_{0<\varepsilon<R<\infty}\|B_{\varepsilon,R}(F,G)\|.$$
- **Trace energy and support** ([source](#sec:matrix)): For a positive semidefinite matrix $P$, set $$\Phi(P)=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(P^{3/2}), \qquad \mathop{\mathrm{supp}}P=(\ker P)^\perp.$$
- **Supported Hessian form** ([source](#sec:matrix)): For Hermitian $K,L$ supported on $P$, define $$\mathcal B_P(K,L) =\frac32\operatorname{Re} \big\langle K, (\mathsf L_{\sqrt P}+\mathsf R_{\sqrt P})^{-1}L \big\rangle_{\mathrm{HS}}, \qquad b_P(K)=\mathcal B_P(K,K).$$
- **Row star** ([source](#eq:row-star)): Combine the two edges incident to $v$ into the *row star* $$\label{eq:row-star} R_v=[\,W_v\quad W_{v-1}^*\,]: H_{v+1}\oplus H_{v-1}\longrightarrow H_v.$$
- **Star Gram matrices** ([source](#eq:star-grams)): Its two Gram matrices are $$\label{eq:star-grams} T_v=R_v^*R_v,\qquad S_v=R_vR_v^*.$$
- **Discrete cubic mass** ([source](#sec:heat)): We also write $$\mathcal S(A)=\sum_{v=0}^2\sum_{i,j=-N}^Nh^2\|A_v(i,j)\|^3.$$
- **Smooth truncation kernel** ([source](#pm-h-23)): For $s>0$ and $0<\varepsilon<R$, define $$k_s(t)=\frac{t}{\sqrt{s}}g_s(t),\qquad \psi=k_1*k_1*k_1,\qquad I_{\varepsilon,R}(t)=\int_{\varepsilon^2}^{R^2} s^{-1/2}\psi(t/\sqrt{s})\,\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm ds}{s}.$$
- **Flat scalar form** ([source](#pm-h-27)): For complex $F_0,F_1,F_2\in L^3(\mathbb R^2)$, define the flat scalar form $$\mathcal L_{\varepsilon,R}(F_0,F_1,F_2) =\iiint_{\varepsilon<\|t\|<R} F_0(x,y)F_1(x+t,y)F_2(x,y+t) \,\mathop{}\!\mathrm dx\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm dy\,\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm dt}{t}.$$

|---|---|---|---|---|
| Q1 | Inserted Gram cost | ≤ | $\frac34\lVert L\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}^2\Phi(R^*R)$ | Only the parameter regime in the cited assertion. |

| Q3 | Neighbor column costs | ≤ | $\sqrt2\,V_w$ | Only the parameter regime in the cited assertion. |
| Q4 | Strict dissipation constant | = | $\frac12\min\{1-\tfrac35\sqrt2,\tfrac{11}{10}-\tfrac12\sqrt2\}$ | Only the parameter regime in the cited assertion. |
| Q5 | Initial energy constant | = | $2\sqrt2\left(1+\sqrt{2/\pi}\right)$ | Only the parameter regime in the cited assertion. |
| Q6 | Strong maximal prefactor | = | $\frac{6q\,2^{q-1}}{q-1}$ | Only the parameter regime in the cited assertion. |
| Q7 | Hard kernel coefficient | = | $-\frac{2}{3\sqrt{6\pi}}$ | Only the parameter regime in the cited assertion. |
| Q8 | Absolute finite truncation norm | ≤ | $2\log(R/\varepsilon)\\|F\\|_3\\|G\\|_3$ | Only the parameter regime in the cited assertion. |

          "exact_quote": "Let $R:E\\to F$ be a linear map between finite-dimensional complex Hilbert spaces, and let $L:F\\to F$ be Hermitian.           "source_id": "PM-082.3"
        }

        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-mixed-trace",
          "exact_quote": "Let $W_v:H_{v+1}\\to H_v$ be linear maps between finite-dimensional complex Hilbert spaces, with row stars $R_v$ and Gram matrices $T_v$ as in <a href=\"#eq:row-star\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:row-star\">[eq:row-star]</a>–<a href=\"#eq:star-grams\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:star-grams\">[eq:star-grams]</a>. For Hermitian maps $D_v:H_v\\to H_v$, put $D_* =\\max_v\\lVert D_v\\rVert_{\\mathrm{op}}$. Then $$\\label{eq:mixed-trace}\n \\left|\\mathop{\\mathrm{tr}}(D_0W_0D_1W_1D_2W_2)\\right|\n \\le \\frac53D_*\\sum_{v=0}^2\n b_{T_v}(R_v^*D_vR_v).$$ The constant is independent of the dimensions of the spaces.",
          "source_id": "PM-082.3"
        }
      ],

        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-plane-dissipation",
          "exact_quote": "Fix finite edge arrays and a positive vector $\\boldsymbol\\alpha$, and evaluate the energies at $\\sigma_i=\\alpha_i s$. Set $$J_v(d,s)=\\int_{\\Pi_d}e_v\\,\\mathop{}\\!\\mathrm d\\pi_d,\n \\qquad J(d,s)=\\sum_vJ_v(d,s),\n \\qquad \\Sigma_\\alpha=\\sum_i\\alpha_i.$$ These integrals are finite, continuously differentiable in $s>0$, and twice continuously differentiable in $d$. Moreover, $$\\label{eq:heat-plane-dissipation}\n 2\\partial_sJ(d,s)\\le\\int_{\\Pi_d}\\sum_w\n \\left[-\\alpha_w+\n \\sqrt2\\max\\{0,\\Sigma_\\alpha/2-\\alpha_w\\}\\right]V_w\\,\\mathop{}\\!\\mathrm d\\pi_d.$$ In particular, for each permutation of $(1,\\lambda,\\lambda)$, $$\\label{eq:heat-strict}\n -\\partial_sJ(d,s)\\ge c_*\\int_{\\Pi_d}\\sum_wV_w\\,\\mathop{}\\!\\mathrm d\\pi_d,\n \\qquad\n c_* = \\frac12\\min\\{1-\\tfrac35\\sqrt2,\\tfrac{11}{10}-\\tfrac12\\sqrt2\\}>0.$$",
          "source_id": "PM-082.3"
        }
      ],
      "fidelity": "PENDING",
      "id": "Q4",
      "kind": "constant",

Moreover, $$\\label{eq:heat-plane-dissipation}\n 2\\partial_sJ(d,s)\\le\\int_{\\Pi_d}\\sum_w\n \\left[-\\alpha_w+\n \\sqrt2\\max\\{0,\\Sigma_\\alpha/2-\\alpha_w\\}\\right]V_w\\,\\mathop{}\\!\\mathrm d\\pi_d.$$ In particular, for each permutation of $(1,\\lambda,\\lambda)$, $$\\label{eq:heat-strict}\n -\\partial_sJ(d,s)\\ge c_*\\int_{\\Pi_d}\\sum_wV_w\\,\\mathop{}\\!\\mathrm d\\pi_d,\n \\qquad\n c_* = \\frac12\\min\\{1-\\tfrac35\\sqrt2,\\tfrac{11}{10}-\\tfrac12\\sqrt2\\}>0.$$",

          "anchor": "stmt-lem-initial-energy",
          "exact_quote": "Let $h>0$, let $N\\ge0$ be an integer, and let $A_v(i,j)$ be the finite complex arrays above. For any $d\\in\\mathbb R$ and variances $\\sigma_v\\ge h^2$, $$\\label{eq:initial-energy}\n \\int_{\\Pi_d}\\sum_ve_v\\,\\mathop{}\\!\\mathrm d\\pi_d\n \\le C_0\\mathcal S(A),\\qquad\n C_0=2\\sqrt2\\left(1+\\sqrt{2/\\pi}\\right).$$ The same bound holds if any entries are replaced by smaller entries in absolute value before forming the weighted matrices.",

          "anchor": "stmt-lem-line-maximal",
          "exact_quote": "On either of these measure spaces, write $M$ for the corresponding centered maximal operator. For $f\\in L^1$ and $\\rho>0$, $$\\mu\\{Mf>\\rho\\}\\le\\frac3\\rho\\|f\\|_1.$$ For every $1<q<\\infty$ and $f\\in L^q$, $$\\label{eq:line-maximal-strong}\n \\|Mf\\|_q^q\\le\n \\frac{6q\\,2^{q-1}}{q-1}\\|f\\|_q^q.$$ Moreover, there is an absolute constant $C_g$ such that, whenever $h>0$, $\\sigma\\ge h^2$ and $n\\in\\mathbb Z$, $$\\label{eq:discrete-gaussian-maximal}\n \\sum_{m\\in\\mathbb Z}h g_\\sigma((m-n)h)|f(m)|\n \\le C_g M_{\\mathbb Z}f(n).$$",
          "source_id": "PM-082.3"
        }

    {

      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-maximal-hard-kernel",
          "exact_quote": "There are an absolute constant $C$ and functions $E_r$, $r>0$, such that, for $t\\ne0$, apart from endpoint values, $$\\label{eq:maximal-hard-decomposition}\n I_{\\varepsilon,R}(t)\n  =\\frac{c_0}{t}\\mathbf1_{\\{\\varepsilon<|t|<R\\}}\n       +E_\\varepsilon(t)-E_R(t),\n \\qquad c_0=-\\frac{2}{3\\sqrt{6\\pi}},$$ and $$\\label{eq:maximal-error-envelope}\n |E_r(t)|\\le\\frac{C}{r}\\left(1+\\frac{|t|}{r}\\right)^{-2}\n \\qquad(t\\ne0).$$",

          "anchor": "stmt-lem-finite-truncations",
          "exact_quote": "For complex $F,G\\in L^3(\\mathbb R^2)$, there is a set of full measure on which every finite truncation is absolutely convergent and depends continuously on $(\\varepsilon,R)$ in $0<\\varepsilon<R<\\infty$. The resulting family is independent of the representatives of $F,G$ up to a common null set. Moreover, $$\\label{eq:elementary-bilinear}\n \\left\\|\\int_{\\varepsilon<|t|<R}\n |F(x+t,y)G(x,y+t)|\\,\\frac{\\mathop{}\\!\\mathrm dt}{|t|}\\right\\|_p\n \\le 2\\log(R/\\varepsilon)\\|F\\|_3\\|G\\|_3.$$",
          "source_id": "PM-082.3"
        }

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **R2** [claim](#stmt-lem-hessian) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-hessian): The energy has a supported Hessian with positivity and a Cauchy–Schwarz bound. -> The energy has a supported Hessian with positivity and a Cauchy–Schwarz bound.
- **R3** [claim](#stmt-lem-order) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-order): Matrix order compares supported Hessian costs in the reverse direction. -> Matrix order compares supported Hessian costs in the reverse direction.
- **R4** [claim](#stmt-lem-insertion) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-insertion): The inserted Gram cost is controlled by the energy and the insertion norm. -> The inserted Gram cost is controlled by the energy and the insertion norm.
- **R5** [claim](#stmt-lem-mixed-trace) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-mixed-trace): The cyclic trace is bounded by star costs independently of the dimensions. -> The cyclic trace is bounded by star costs independently of the dimensions.
- **R6** [claim](#stmt-lem-star-smoothness) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-star-smoothness): Positive Gaussian weights give smooth star energies with the stated fixed supports. -> Positive Gaussian weights give smooth star energies with the stated fixed supports.
- **R7** [claim](#stmt-lem-heat-identities) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-heat-identities): Heat differentiation separates center derivatives from score costs. -> Heat differentiation separates center derivatives from score costs.
- **R8** [claim](#stmt-lem-neighbors) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-neighbors): The neighboring column costs are controlled by the row score cost. -> The neighboring column costs are controlled by the row score cost.
- **R9** [claim](#stmt-lem-plane-dissipation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-plane-dissipation): Plane integration yields strict dissipation for the specified variance rates. -> Plane integration yields strict dissipation for the specified variance rates.
- **R10** [claim](#stmt-lem-initial-energy) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-initial-energy): Variances no smaller than the mesh scale give a uniform initial energy bound. -> Variances no smaller than the mesh scale give a uniform initial energy bound.
- **R11** [claim](#stmt-lem-line-maximal) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-line-maximal): Centered maximal estimates control continuous and discrete line averages. -> Centered maximal estimates control continuous and discrete line averages.
- **R12** [claim](#stmt-lem-rank-change) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-rank-change): The energy variation formula remains valid through rank changes. -> The energy variation formula remains valid through rank changes.
- **R13** [claim](#stmt-prop-masked-dissipation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-masked-dissipation): Independent entry endpoints preserve a uniform bound on accumulated dissipation. -> Independent entry endpoints preserve a uniform bound on accumulated dissipation.
- **R14** [claim](#stmt-prop-selected-kernel) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-selected-kernel): The finite mesh estimate allows independently selected smooth endpoints. -> The finite mesh estimate allows independently selected smooth endpoints.
- **R15** [claim](#stmt-prop-smooth-kernel-maximal) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-smooth-kernel-maximal): Finite maxima and mesh limits give the smooth kernel maximal estimate. -> Finite maxima and mesh limits give the smooth kernel maximal estimate.

More precisely, $$\\label{eq:maximal-tail-convergence}\n \\left\\|\\sup_{\\substack{0<\\varepsilon<1/n\\\\R>n}}\n |B_{\\varepsilon,R}(F,G)-B(F,G)|\\right\\|_{3/2}\n \\longrightarrow0.$$ The limit is the unique bounded bilinear extension of the principal-value operator on compactly supported smooth inputs.",
          "source_id": "PM-082.3"
        }

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **I-coordinate**: The explicit flat and simplex coordinate maps preserve measure and identify the integrands and truncation regions.
- **I-maximal**: The hard-truncation supremum lies inside the spatial norm and controls both endpoints.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **A-finite**: Treat exact coordinate checks as a certificate of the maximal estimate or principal-value convergence. -> Coordinate algebra does not establish analytic integrability, maximal domination, or convergence.
- **A-constant**: Identify the optimal pointwise maximal constant with the optimal scalar constant. -> Equality of flat and simplex scalar constants does not establish equality with the pointwise maximal constant.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `constructive`. The scalar-equivalence proof supplies explicit finite coordinate maps and their inverses. The executable contract checks their algebra and hard truncation regions; it does not decide the analytic bounds or principal values.

```python
# INPUTS: JSON list of rational [a,b,c,epsilon,R] rows, at most 1000 rows.
# PRECONDITIONS: Every row has five rational strings and 0 < epsilon < R.
# OUTPUTS: Exact flat coordinates, inverse coordinates, pair arguments, determinants, and hard-mask membership.
# TIME: Linear in the number of rows in exact arithmetic operations.
# SPACE: Linear in the number of rows and rational bit lengths.
# ARITHMETIC: Exact python-flint fmpq and fmpq_mat arithmetic.
# FAILURE: Reject malformed rows, invalid endpoints, or more than 1000 rows.
import json, sys
from flint import fmpq, fmpq_mat
rows = json.loads(sys.stdin.read())
assert isinstance(rows,list) and len(rows)<=1000
M = fmpq_mat([[-1,0,0],[0,-1,0],[1,1,1]])
P0 = fmpq_mat([[-1,0],[0,-1]])
P1 = fmpq_mat([[1,1],[-1,0]])
P2 = fmpq_mat([[0,-1],[1,1]])
answer=[]
for row in rows:
    assert len(row)==5
    a,b,c,lo,hi = map(fmpq,row)
    assert 0 < lo < hi
    flat = M * fmpq_mat([[a],[b],[c]])
    x,y,t = [flat[i,0] for i in range(3)]
    back = M.inv() * flat
    p0 = P0 * fmpq_mat([[a],[b]])
    p1 = P1 * fmpq_mat([[b],[c]])
    p2 = P2 * fmpq_mat([[c],[a]])
    values=[x,y,t]+[back[i,0] for i in range(3)]+[p[i,0] for p in (p0,p1,p2) for i in range(2)]
    dets=[M.det(),P0.det(),P1.det(),P2.det()]
    answer.append({'values':list(map(str,values)), 'determinants':list(map(str,dets)), 'inside':bool(lo<abs(t)<hi)})
print(json.dumps(answer,separators=(',',':')))
```

## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | True | moderate | adaptable | inferred | A matrix-analysis component can use the entrywise energy-gradient bound to audit changes of finite complex matrices, including rank changes. Transfer requires that the component computes the stated Gram energy and preserves the matrix hypotheses; the paper gives no software accuracy or execution guarantee. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The transform estimate concerns translated complex functions on the plane and Lebesgue norms. It supplies no simulation dynamics, collision model, or time-stepping contract. |
| tools | True | moderate | adaptable | inferred | A diagnostic for finite matrix pipelines can compare an inserted Gram cost with the Gram energy when the insertion is Hermitian. This is a conditional analytic check; a general matrix pipeline must first satisfy these hypotheses. |
| algorithms | True | moderate | adaptable | inferred | Finite endpoint selection is constructive: choose a maximizing output at each mesh pair, breaking ties by the least index. The resulting bound applies to the specified cyclic array expression and smooth truncation kernels; it does not certify a general cutoff-search algorithm. |
| data_structures | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The row star packages the incident edge maps into a block matrix for trace-energy analysis. This gives a mathematical representation, but no storage interface, update complexity, or data-structure performance theorem. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The finite matrix inequalities have dimension-independent analytic constants. No accelerator execution model or memory-traffic estimate is established for evaluating these matrices. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The stated contract is a maximal singular-integral bound for complex functions. It does not provide a cryptographic construction, adversary model, or security reduction. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The matrix energy and transform bound are proved for the stated analytic objects. No learning objective, training procedure, sample model, or generalisation guarantee is supplied. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Energy variation along matrix interpolation is controlled, but this is not an optimisation contract: no feasible-set model, minimiser guarantee, or optimisation iteration is established. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Endpoint selection may vary with output position, but the arrays and analytic truncation family do not define a distributed execution or communication model. |
| other (harmonic_analysis) | True | strong | adaptable | stated | Harmonic analysis directly uses the maximal triangular Hilbert transform estimate and its joint principal value. Transfer requires the exact translated-product operator and the stated complex Lebesgue input spaces; the bound controls both hard endpoints inside the spatial norm. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **input-regime** (hypothesis): The maximal estimate requires complex inputs in the stated Lebesgue space on the plane. Transfer to other exponents, measures, or translation patterns requires a separate argument. Blocked use: ['Bounds for different input exponents or different singular integral operators.']
- **maximal-versus-individual** (edge_case): The supremum over both hard truncation endpoints occurs inside the spatial norm. A uniform bound for individual truncations does not supply this pointwise maximal conclusion. Blocked use: ['Replacing the pointwise supremum by a supremum of individual norms in the proof.']
- **supported-hessian** (hypothesis): The Hessian formulas use Hermitian directions on a fixed positive support. Kernel directions cannot be included by treating a singular Gram matrix as invertible. Blocked use: ['Applying the inverse Hessian operator to unsupported directions.']
- **order-precondition** (hypothesis): The order comparison assumes positive semidefinite matrices on the same space and the stated quadratic-form domination. Entrywise comparison does not establish this hypothesis. Blocked use: ['Using entrywise matrix order in place of quadratic-form order.']
- **strict-variance-regime** (hypothesis): The strict heat dissipation conclusion uses the specified permutations of the variance rates. Positivity of arbitrary variance rates alone does not supply the asserted strict coefficient. Blocked use: ['Applying the strict dissipation estimate to arbitrary positive variance rates.']
- **mesh-scale** (hypothesis): The initial-energy estimate requires each variance to be no smaller than the squared mesh. The proof does not provide this bound uniformly at smaller variance scales. Blocked use: ['Using the mesh-independent initial-energy constant below the stated variance threshold.']
- **endpoint-switching** (hypothesis): Each masked entry has one fixed opening endpoint and one fixed closing endpoint, independent of the Gaussian centers. The total-jump argument does not justify unrestricted repeated switching or center-dependent selection. Blocked use: ['Using the same jump bound for arbitrary repeated toggles or center-dependent endpoints.']
- **rank-change** (edge_case): The variation formula permits rank changes but requires finite rectangular endpoint matrices of the same size. It is an analytic integral identity, without a numerical integration error guarantee. Blocked use: ['Applying the formula to matrices with different dimensions or treating an approximate integral as exact.']
- **mesh-limit** (approximation): The continuum passage fixes a finite endpoint family and compact smooth inputs before passing from Riemann sums to their limits. It supplies convergence in that setting, without a finite-mesh error rate. Blocked use: ['Claiming a certified finite-grid approximation rate for general Lebesgue inputs.']
- **common-null-set** (edge_case): For general inputs the truncations and their endpoint continuity are defined outside a common null set. The conclusions do not assert convergence at every spatial point for arbitrary representatives. Blocked use: ['Requiring pointwise convergence everywhere for arbitrary representatives.']
- **joint-limit-rate** (nonconstructive): The joint principal value exists almost everywhere and in the stated norm. This convergence statement gives no explicit finite choice of endpoints achieving a prescribed approximation error for arbitrary inputs. Blocked use: ['Treating principal-value existence as a finite stopping criterion with a prescribed error.']
- **scalar-maximal-constant** (edge_case): Equality of optimal constants is asserted for the uniform flat and simplex scalar estimates. It does not identify their common scalar constant with the pointwise maximal optimal constant. Blocked use: ['Identifying the scalar optimal constant with the pointwise maximal optimal constant.']
- **exact-coordinate-subroutine** (formal_scope): The coordinate changes preserve the stated forms and norms. Checking their finite algebraic identities alone does not certify the analytic maximal estimate or the principal-value limit. Blocked use: ['Promoting a finite coordinate-map check to a certificate of the continuum theorem.']

### Formal statement (Lean 4)

Corpus status: formalized in the source corpus (toolchain `leanprover/lean4:v4.34.1`, Mathlib `d13f23b723b8a846827a245b89c10fc7d3f11612`). Not re-checked here; the statements give the exact quantifiers and hypotheses, not a proof.

Scope note of family 082 (`lean/docs/082.md`, verbatim):

> The following describes the scope of the Lean formalization related to the following accompanying paper(s):
>
> - [The maximal triangular Hilbert transform at the symmetric point](../../preprints/The-maximal-triangular-Hilbert-transform-at-the-symmetric-point-September-24-2026/paper.pdf)
>
> ## Scope
>
> The formalized result proves the symmetric maximal estimate for the triangular Hilbert transform: for arbitrary complex $F,G\in L^3(\mathbb R^2)$, the $L^{3/2}$ norm of the supremum over all finite hard-truncation intervals is at most $C\|F\|_3\|G\|_3$ for one absolute constant $C$. It also establishes a common full-measure set on which the truncated integrals are defined and almost-everywhere measurability of the maximal output.

Comparator source `TriangularHilbert.lean` (1471 bytes, sha256 `768eef032a27b868cbeba17c167787a2183aacb6368c2d1733b400d55d264acc`), verbatim:

```lean
import Mathlib

namespace OAI

noncomputable section

open MeasureTheory
open scoped ENNReal NNReal

namespace TriangularHilbert

abbrev Plane := ℝ × ℝ

structure Endpoints where
  lower : ℝ
  upper : ℝ
  lower_pos : 0 < lower
  lower_lt_upper : lower < upper

def annulus (ε R : ℝ) : Set ℝ := {t | ε < |t| ∧ |t| < R}

def integrand (F G : Plane → ℂ) (z : Plane) (t : ℝ) : ℂ :=
  F (z.1 + t, z.2) * G (z.1, z.2 + t) / (t : ℂ)

def truncation (F G : Plane → ℂ) (E : Endpoints) (z : Plane) : ℂ :=
  ∫ t in annulus E.lower E.upper, integrand F G z t

def GoodPoint (F G : Plane → ℂ) (z : Plane) : Prop :=
  ∀ n : ℕ, IntegrableOn (integrand F G z)
    (annulus (1 / ((n : ℝ) + 2)) ((n : ℝ) + 2))

def maximal (F G : Plane → ℂ) (z : Plane) : ℝ≥0∞ := by
  classical
  exact if GoodPoint F G z then
    ⨆ E : Endpoints, ENNReal.ofReal ‖truncation F G E z‖ else 0

def maximalNorm (F G : Plane → ℂ) : ℝ≥0∞ :=
  (∫⁻ z : Plane, maximal F G z ^ ((3 : ℝ) / 2)) ^ ((2 : ℝ) / 3)

def MainEstimate : Prop :=
  ∃ C : ℝ≥0, ∀ F G : Plane → ℂ,
    MemLp F 3 volume → MemLp G 3 volume →
    (∀ᵐ z ∂volume, GoodPoint F G z) ∧
    AEMeasurable (maximal F G) volume ∧
    maximalNorm F G ≤ (C : ℝ≥0∞) * eLpNorm F 3 volume * eLpNorm G 3 volume

open MeasureTheory Filter
open scoped ENNReal

theorem main_estimate : MainEstimate := by
  sorry

end TriangularHilbert

end

end OAI
```

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


For arbitrary complex inputs in $`L^3(\mathbb R^2)`$, we prove the pointwise maximal $`L^3\times L^3\to L^{3/2}`$ estimate for the triangular Hilbert transform, with the supremum over both hard truncation endpoints. The estimate yields joint almost-everywhere and <i>L</i><sup>3/2</sup> convergence as the lower endpoint tends to zero and the upper endpoint tends to infinity. This also proves the conjectured scalar estimate at the symmetric point.

<a id="pm-h-1"></a>
#### Statements index

| anchor | kind | label | number | first sentence |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| [stmt-thm-main](#stmt-thm-main) | theorem | `thm:main` | 1 | There is an absolute constant $C$ such that, for every pair of complex functions $F,G\in L^3(\mathbb R^2)$, |
| [stmt-lem-hessian](#stmt-lem-hessian) | lemma | `lem:hessian` | 2 | On the cone of matrices positive definite on a fixed support, $\Phi$ is smooth. |
| [stmt-lem-order](#stmt-lem-order) | lemma | `lem:order` | 3 | Let $P,Q$ be positive semidefinite matrices on the same Hilbert space, let $a\ge1$, and suppose $P\le aQ$. |

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

The triangular Hilbert transform couples translations in two different coordinate directions. For complex functions $F,G$ on $\mathbb R^2$ and $0<\varepsilon<R<\infty$, write $$B_{\varepsilon,R}(F,G)(x,y)
 =\int_{\varepsilon<|t|<R}F(x+t,y)G(x,y+t)\,\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm dt}{t},
 \qquad
 B_*(F,G)=\sup_{0<\varepsilon<R<\infty}|B_{\varepsilon,R}(F,G)|.$$ For $L^3$ inputs, the finite integrals are absolutely convergent outside one common null set, and the supremum is measurable; the details are given in Lemma <a href="#lem:finite-truncations" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:finite-truncations">19</a>.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *There is an absolute constant $C$ such that, for every pair of complex functions $F,G\in L^3(\mathbb R^2)$, $$\|B_*(F,G)\|_{L^{3/2}(\mathbb R^2)}
 \le C\|F\|_{L^3(\mathbb R^2)}\|G\|_{L^3(\mathbb R^2)}.$$ The supremum is over both finite hard truncation endpoints.*

</div>

The estimate controls a truncation chosen separately at each spatial point. This is stronger than a uniform bound for the norm of each individual truncation: the same $L^{3/2}$ majorant must control every choice of the two endpoints. It also gives the joint principal value $$B(F,G)=\lim_{\substack{\varepsilon\downarrow0\\R\uparrow\infty}}
 B_{\varepsilon,R}(F,G)$$ almost everywhere and in $L^{3/2}$, with the same norm bound (Theorem <a href="#thm:bilinear-pv" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:bilinear-pv">20</a>).

Pairing the bilinear output with a third $L^3$ function leads to the scalar triangular form. In its symmetric coordinates this is $$\Lambda_{\varepsilon,R}(G_0,G_1,G_2)
 =\iiint_{\varepsilon<|a+b+c|<R}
 G_0(a,b)G_1(b,c)G_2(c,a)
 \,\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm da\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm db\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm dc}{a+b+c}.$$ The three factors have equal exponent $3$, which is the symmetric point of the scalar Hölder relation $1/p_0+1/p_1+1/p_2=1$. We obtain an endpoint-uniform bound by $C\prod_v\|G_v\|_3$ and a joint scalar principal value for every complex $L^3$ triple. Proposition <a href="#prop:flat-equivalence" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:flat-equivalence">22</a> gives the explicit determinant-one changes of variables and proves that the flat and simplex scalar estimates have the same optimal constant. In particular, the result resolves positively the symmetric triangular Hilbert transform problem stated by Thiele in (Grafakos et al. 2017, sec. 9, Problem 13) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[GrafakosEtAl2017](#ref-GrafakosEtAl2017)].

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Previous work

The flat operator appears in Demeter and Thiele’s discussion of bilinear averages for two commuting transformations (Demeter and Thiele 2010, sec. 6) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[DemeterThiele2010](#ref-DemeterThiele2010)]. The three inputs of the scalar form depend on the three pairs of variables, making it a basic example of an entangled singular integral. Kovač, Thiele and Zorin-Kranich proved estimates for a Walsh model with one structurally restricted input (Kovač, Thiele, and Zorin-Kranich 2015, Theorem 1.7) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[KovacThieleZorinKranich2015](#ref-KovacThieleZorinKranich2015)].

Tao proved sublogarithmic cancellation for one-dimensional multilinear Hilbert transforms (Tao 2016, Theorem 1.2) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[Tao2016Cancellation](#ref-Tao2016Cancellation)]. Zorin-Kranich extended this cancellation to simplex Hilbert transforms (Zorin-Kranich 2017, Theorem 1.3) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[ZorinKranich2017](#ref-ZorinKranich2017)]. Square-function estimates of Durcik, Kovač, Škreb and Thiele gave square-root cancellation for the triangular form across finitely many smooth scales (Durcik et al. 2019, Corollary 5) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[DurcikKovacSkrebThiele2019](#ref-DurcikKovacSkrebThiele2019)]. Durcik, Kovač and Thiele proved power-type cancellation with hard truncations (Durcik, Kovač, and Thiele 2019, Theorem 1) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[DurcikKovacThiele2019](#ref-DurcikKovacThiele2019)]; at the symmetric tuple the bound grows like $\sqrt{\log(R/\varepsilon)}$, by permutation and interpolation of their $(4,4,2)$ estimates. These finite-scale bounds retain a dependence on the scale range.

Bounds for averages over direction parameters were obtained by Durcik and Roos (Durcik and Roos 2021, Theorem 1) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[DurcikRoos2021](#ref-DurcikRoos2021)] and, for rough angular weights, by Lin and Slavíková (Lin and Slavíková 2026, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[LinSlavikova2026](#ref-LinSlavikova2026)]. For curved translations, Christ, Durcik and Roos proved bounds along $(t,t^2)$ (Christ, Durcik, and Roos 2021, Theorem 1) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[ChristDurcikRoos2021](#ref-ChristDurcikRoos2021)]. Hsu and Lin treat more general curves under geometric hypotheses excluding the flat curve $(t,t)$ (Hsu and Lin 2026, Theorem 1.37) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[HsuLin2026](#ref-HsuLin2026)]. The curvature in these results provides an additional source of cancellation that is unavailable for the flat curve.

Our argument uses a nonnegative matrix energy and Gaussian heat flow. Energy telescoping for entangled forms appears in Kovač’s twisted-paraproduct argument (Kovač 2012, Lemma 2) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[Kovac2012Twisted](#ref-Kovac2012Twisted)] and his Bellman-function framework (Kovač 2011, sec. 2) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[Kovac2011](#ref-Kovac2011)]. Continuous Gaussian telescoping appears in Durcik (Durcik 2015, Lemma 3 and Section 3) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[Durcik2015](#ref-Durcik2015)]. These methods concern bipartite forms; Kovač discusses the obstacle posed by the triangular cycle in (Kovač 2011, sec. 6.2) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[Kovac2011](#ref-Kovac2011)]. The cyclic trace representation itself also occurs in (Kovač, Thiele, and Zorin-Kranich 2015, Equation (1.5)) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[KovacThieleZorinKranich2015](#ref-KovacThieleZorinKranich2015)]. We prove a dimension-independent mixed trace inequality and compare the energy derivatives at neighboring vertices. The key additional estimate controls the total change in energy when individual matrix entries are switched on and off at independently chosen scales. This permits the pointwise maximum without a factor depending on the number of scales or the matrix dimensions.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### Proof overview

The change of variables $x=-a$, $y=-b$, $t=a+b+c$ writes the bilinear output in terms of $$G_1(b,c)=F(b+c,-b),\qquad G_2(c,a)=G(-a,c+a).$$ Both pair maps preserve $L^3$ norms. We first sample these inputs, together with a dual input $G_0(a,b)$, on a finite mesh. Multiplying entries by Gaussian weights gives maps $W_v:H_{v+1}\to H_v$ between finite-dimensional complex Hilbert spaces, indexed by $v\in\mathbb Z/3\mathbb Z$. The three vertex spaces correspond to the sampled $a$-, $b$- and $c$-coordinates. Thus the two indices of the dual array record the output position $(x,y)=(-a,-b)$. At a vertex, combine its two incident maps into $R_v=[\,W_v\quad W_{v-1}^*\,]$ and use the energy $\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}((R_v^*R_v)^{3/2})$.

Sections <a href="#sec:matrix" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:matrix">2</a> and <a href="#sec:trace" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:trace">3</a> prove the matrix inequalities. Section <a href="#sec:heat" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:heat">4</a> then integrates the sum of the three energies over Gaussian centers satisfying $p_0+p_1+p_2=0$. For fixed arrays, its decrease as the variance grows controls the quadratic costs needed to estimate a cyclic trace with three odd Gaussian windows.

To select different truncations at different output points, the dual array entry at $(i,j)$ must be present only between its own two scales. The energy is therefore decreasing between finitely many switching scales and may jump at those scales. Section <a href="#sec:jumps" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:jumps">5</a> bounds the sum of the absolute jumps by the discrete $L^3$ masses of the three arrays. The underlying matrix fact is that the derivative with respect to an entry is controlled by the product of its row and column norms. After Gaussian integration, one-dimensional maximal functions bound these products uniformly in the switching scales.

Section <a href="#sec:maximal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:maximal">6</a> uses this bound to linearize any finite maximum of smooth truncations. Mesh limits and rational exhaustion give the full smooth maximum. A pointwise comparison of its kernel with the hard Hilbert kernel completes the estimate for compactly supported smooth inputs. Finally, Section <a href="#sec:consequences" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:consequences">7</a> uses density and a vanishing tail-oscillation argument to prove Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>, the joint principal values, and the scalar equivalence.

<a id="sec:matrix"></a>
### A matrix energy and its Hessian

We begin with the finite-dimensional inequalities that control the derivatives of our energy. Their constants are independent of the matrix dimensions. Throughout this section, matrices act on finite-dimensional complex Hilbert spaces, and inequalities between Hermitian matrices mean inequalities of quadratic forms.

For a positive semidefinite matrix $P$, set $$\Phi(P)=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(P^{3/2}),
 \qquad \mathop{\mathrm{supp}}P=(\ker P)^\perp.$$ A matrix is *supported on $P$* if it vanishes on $\ker P$ on both sides. We use the Hilbert–Schmidt inner product $\langle K,L\rangle_{\mathrm{HS}}=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(K^*L)$. For a Hermitian matrix $A$, write $\mathsf L_A(X)=AX$ and $\mathsf R_A(X)=XA$. On matrices acting on $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}P$, the operator $\mathsf L_{\sqrt P}+\mathsf R_{\sqrt P}$ is positive definite. For Hermitian $K,L$ supported on $P$, define $$\mathcal B_P(K,L)
 =\frac32\operatorname{Re}
   \big\langle K,
    (\mathsf L_{\sqrt P}+\mathsf R_{\sqrt P})^{-1}L
   \big\rangle_{\mathrm{HS}},
 \qquad b_P(K)=\mathcal B_P(K,K).$$ If $P=0$, the only supported directions are zero, and these expressions are defined to be zero.

The square-root derivative used here is a case of the classical divided-difference calculus for Hermitian matrix functions (Daletskii 1957, sec. 1, Equation (1)) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[Daletskii1957](#ref-Daletskii1957)]. We give a direct inverse-function proof to keep the support conventions explicit.

<a id="stmt-lem-hessian"></a>
<div id="lem:hessian" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:hessian-metric"></a>
**Lemma 2** (Energy derivatives). *On the cone of matrices positive definite on a fixed support, $\Phi$ is smooth. For Hermitian directions $K,L$ on that support, $$d\Phi_P(K)=\frac32\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(\sqrt P K),
 \qquad
 d^2\Phi_P(K,L)=\mathcal B_P(K,L).$$ If $p_i>0$ are the eigenvalues on the support, then in an orthonormal eigenbasis $$\label{eq:hessian-metric}
 b_P(K)=\frac32\sum_{i,j}
          \frac{|K_{ij}|^2}{\sqrt{p_i}+\sqrt{p_j}}.$$ In particular, $\mathcal B_P$ is a positive definite symmetric bilinear form on the real space of supported Hermitian directions, and $$|\mathcal B_P(K,L)|\le b_P(K)^{1/2}b_P(L)^{1/2}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-hessian"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-hessian</summary>

*Proof.* Restrict to the support and put $A=\sqrt P$. The derivative of the squaring map at $A$ is $X\mapsto AX+XA$. In an eigenbasis of $A$, this map multiplies its $(i,j)$ entry by $\sqrt{p_i}+\sqrt{p_j}>0$. The inverse function theorem therefore gives a smooth positive square root. Its derivative $X_K$ in direction $K$ satisfies $$AX_K+X_KA=K.$$ Taking a trace after multiplication by $A$ yields $\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(PX_K)=\tfrac12\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(AK)$. Differentiation of $\Phi(P)=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(P\sqrt P)$ now gives $$d\Phi_P(K)=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(KA)+\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(PX_K)=\frac32\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(AK).$$ Differentiating this identity in direction $L$ proves the Hessian formula. The entrywise formula follows by diagonalizing $A$. It also proves symmetry, positivity, and Cauchy–Schwarz on the real space of Hermitian directions. ◻

</details>

The energy and its Hessian are unchanged by simultaneous unitary conjugation of their arguments. They are also unchanged if all arguments are extended by zero to a larger Hilbert space: the positive eigenvalues and the supported entries in <a href="#eq:hessian-metric" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hessian-metric">[eq:hessian-metric]</a> stay the same. We will use these observations to compare Grams acting on different subspaces.

<a id="stmt-lem-order"></a>
<div id="lem:order" class="lemma">

**Lemma 3** (Order comparison). *Let $P,Q$ be positive semidefinite matrices on the same Hilbert space, let $a\ge1$, and suppose $P\le aQ$. Every Hermitian $K$ supported on $P$ is supported on $Q$, and <a id="eq:matrix-order"></a>$$\label{eq:matrix-order}
 b_P(K)\ge a^{-1/2}b_Q(K).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-order"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-order</summary>

*Proof.* If $x\in\ker Q$, then $0\le\langle Px,x\rangle
\le a\langle Qx,x\rangle=0$, so $Px=0$. Thus $\ker Q\subseteq\ker P$, proving the support assertion.

We recall the two operator-order facts needed for the comparison. Inversion reverses order on positive definite matrices: for $0<X\le Y$, conjugate by $X^{-1/2}$, invert the resulting matrix at least $I$, and conjugate back. Moreover, $$\sqrt X=\frac1\pi\int_0^\infty
       X(X+tI)^{-1}\,\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm dt}{\sqrt t}.$$ This norm-convergent formula follows from the scalar integral by spectral calculus. Since $X(X+tI)^{-1}=I-t(X+tI)^{-1}$, inversion order proves that the positive square root preserves order. This is the classical Loewner–Heinz square-root monotonicity; see, for example, Kwong (Kwong 1975, Theorem 1 and Section 2) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[Kwong1975](#ref-Kwong1975)]. The supported Hessian comparison below is derived from these order facts here.

For $\eta>0$, the assumptions give $$P+\eta I\le a(Q+\eta I),
 \qquad
 \sqrt{P+\eta I}\le\sqrt a\,\sqrt{Q+\eta I}.$$ If $A\le B$, then $\mathsf L_A+\mathsf R_A\le\mathsf L_B+\mathsf R_B$ as selfadjoint operators on Hilbert–Schmidt space. Indeed, left and right multiplication by the positive matrix $B-A$ have the nonnegative quadratic forms $\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(X^*(B-A)X)$ and $\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(X^*X(B-A))$. Inverting the resulting positive definite operators gives $$b_{P+\eta I}(K)\ge a^{-1/2}b_{Q+\eta I}(K).$$ Finally let $\eta\downarrow0$. Formula <a href="#eq:hessian-metric" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hessian-metric">[eq:hessian-metric]</a>, applied in a full eigenbasis, shows that both limits are the asserted supported costs: every entry of $K$ meeting the kernel of the respective matrix is zero. ◻

</details>

We will apply the Hessian to Gram matrices with an insertion between the two factors. The following bound controls such derivatives by the energy itself.

<a id="stmt-lem-insertion"></a>
<div id="lem:insertion" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:insertion-bound"></a>
**Lemma 4** (Inserted Gram bound). *Let $R:E\to F$ be a linear map between finite-dimensional complex Hilbert spaces, and let $L:F\to F$ be Hermitian. The matrix $R^*LR$ is supported on $R^*R$, and $$\label{eq:insertion-bound}
 b_{R^*R}(R^*LR)
 \le\frac34\lVert L\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}^2\Phi(R^*R).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-insertion"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-insertion</summary>

*Proof.* The insertion annihilates $\ker R=\ker(R^*R)$ and is Hermitian, so it is supported on the Gram. The assertion is immediate if $R=0$. Otherwise take a singular-value decomposition, with positive singular values $\rho_i$, and let $C$ be the compression of $L$ to the corresponding left singular vectors. Then $\lVert C\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\le\lVert L\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}$ and $$b_{R^*R}(R^*LR)
 =\frac32\sum_{i,j}
   \frac{\rho_i^2\rho_j^2}{\rho_i+\rho_j}|C_{ij}|^2.$$ For $x,y>0$, the arithmetic–geometric mean inequality gives $$\frac{x^2y^2}{x+y}
 \le\frac{(xy)^{3/2}}2
 \le\frac{x^3+y^3}4.$$ Each row and each column of $C$ has squared Euclidean norm at most $\lVert L\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}^2$. Consequently $$b_{R^*R}(R^*LR)
 \le\frac38\sum_{i,j}(\rho_i^3+\rho_j^3)|C_{ij}|^2
 \le\frac34\lVert L\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}^2\sum_i\rho_i^3,$$ which is <a href="#eq:insertion-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:insertion-bound">[eq:insertion-bound]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:trace"></a>
### A mixed trace inequality

The triangular form is naturally represented by a cyclic product of operators. Such a representation also appears in the dyadic model of Kovač, Thiele and Zorin-Kranich (Kovač, Thiele, and Zorin-Kranich 2015, Equation (1.5)) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[KovacThieleZorinKranich2015](#ref-KovacThieleZorinKranich2015)]. We prove a finite-dimensional estimate that controls this product by three of the quadratic costs from Section <a href="#sec:matrix" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:matrix">2</a>.

<a id="eq:row-star"></a>
<a id="eq:star-grams"></a>
Let $H_v$, $v\in\mathbb Z/3\mathbb Z$, be finite-dimensional complex Hilbert spaces, and let $W_v:H_{v+1}\to H_v$ be arbitrary linear maps. Combine the two edges incident to $v$ into the *row star* $$\label{eq:row-star}
 R_v=[\,W_v\quad W_{v-1}^*\,]:
 H_{v+1}\oplus H_{v-1}\longrightarrow H_v.$$ Its two Gram matrices are $$\label{eq:star-grams}
 T_v=R_v^*R_v,\qquad S_v=R_vR_v^*.$$ Their positive eigenvalues are the squares of the nonzero singular values of $R_v$, with the same multiplicities. In particular, $\Phi(T_v)=\Phi(S_v)$. Figure <a href="#fig:triangle" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:triangle">1</a> shows the cyclic edges and the row star at vertex $0$.

<figure id="fig:triangle">

<figcaption>The three edge maps and the row star at vertex $0$. Taking the adjoint of $W_{2}$ makes both blocks of $R_{0}$ have target $H_{0}$. The two Grams $R_{0}^{*}R_{0}$ and $R_{0}R_{0}^{*}$ have the same positive eigenvalues on different spaces.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-triangle"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:triangle`)

 \node[space] (h1) at (-1.8,-.8) {$H_1$};
 \node[space] (h2) at (1.8,-.8) {$H_2$};
 \draw[edge] (h1) -- node[left=5pt] {$W_0$} (h0);
 \draw[edge] (h2) -- node[below=5pt] {$W_1$} (h1);
 \draw[edge] (h0) -- node[right=5pt] {$W_2$} (h2);
 \node[below=18pt] at (0,-.8) {the cyclic product $W_0W_1W_2$};
 \node[space] (r1) at (5,1.3) {$H_1$};
 \node[space] (r2) at (5,-.8) {$H_2$};
 \node[space] (r0) at (7.5,.25) {$H_0$};
 \draw[edge] (r1) -- node[above=5pt] {$W_0$} (r0);
 \draw[edge] (r2) -- node[below=5pt] {$W_2^*$} (r0);
