Visual witness description of PDF page 6 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

```text
Two diagrams side by side:
- Left: A directed triangle with vertices $H_0$ (top), $H_1$ (bottom-left), and $H_2$ (bottom-right). Blue directed edges: $W_0$ pointing from $H_1$ to $H_0$, $W_1$ pointing from $H_2$ to $H_1$, and $W_2$ pointing from $H_0$ to $H_2$. Subtitle label: "the cyclic product $W_0W_1W_2$".
- Right: A graph with vertices $H_1$ (top-left), $H_2$ (bottom-left), and $H_0$ (right). Blue directed edges: $W_0$ pointing from $H_1$ to $H_0$, and $W_2^*$ pointing from $H_2$ to $H_0$. Subtitle label: "$R_0 = [ W_0 \quad W_2^* ]$".
```


<a id="stmt-lem-mixed-trace"></a>
<div id="lem:mixed-trace" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:mixed-trace"></a>
**Lemma 5** (Mixed trace estimate). *Let $W_v:H_{v+1}\to H_v$ be linear maps between finite-dimensional complex Hilbert spaces, with row stars $R_v$ and Gram matrices $T_v$ as in <a href="#eq:row-star" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:row-star">[eq:row-star]</a>–<a href="#eq:star-grams" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:star-grams">[eq:star-grams]</a>. For Hermitian maps $D_v:H_v\to H_v$, put $D_* =\max_v\lVert D_v\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}$. Then $$\label{eq:mixed-trace}
 \left|\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(D_0W_0D_1W_1D_2W_2)\right|
 \le \frac53D_*\sum_{v=0}^2
 b_{T_v}(R_v^*D_vR_v).$$ The constant is independent of the dimensions of the spaces.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-mixed-trace"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-mixed-trace</summary>

*Proof.* We first put the three quadratic costs in a common metric. We then estimate a cubic trace in an eigenbasis and recover the absolute value of the original cyclic product by changing the phase of one edge.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
###### A common quadratic form.

On $H=H_0\oplus H_1\oplus H_2$, define the Hermitian matrices $$M=\begin{pmatrix}
 0&W_0&W_2^*\\
 W_0^*&0&W_1\\
 W_2&W_1^*&0
 \end{pmatrix},
 \qquad D=\mathop{\mathrm{diag}}(D_0,D_1,D_2).$$ Thus $\lVert D\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}=D_*$. Let $P_v$ be the orthogonal projection onto $H_v$ and set $$A_v=MP_vM,\qquad K_v=MP_vDM.$$ Only the $v$-th block row of $P_vM$ can be nonzero; it consists of $R_v$ with its columns placed in the corresponding vertex spaces. Consequently $A_v$ and $K_v$ are, respectively, $T_v$ and $R_v^*D_vR_v$, after a permutation of blocks and extension by zero. These operations preserve the quadratic cost. Since $P_v$ commutes with $D$, the matrix $K_v$ is Hermitian. Moreover, $$\ker A_v=\ker(P_vM)\subseteq\ker K_v,
 \qquad 0\le A_v\le M^2.$$ The first identity follows from $\langle A_vx,x\rangle=\lVert P_vMx\rVert^2$. Thus $K_v$ is supported on $A_v$, and hence also on $M^2$. Lemma <a href="#lem:order" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:order">3</a>, followed by the quadratic-form inequality for three vectors, yields <a id="eq:trace-common-metric"></a>$$\label{eq:trace-common-metric}
 \begin{aligned}
 \mathcal E
 &:=\sum_v b_{T_v}(R_v^*D_vR_v)\\
 &\ge\sum_v b_{M^2}(K_v)
 \ge\frac13 b_{M^2}\left(\sum_vK_v\right)
 =\frac13 b_{M^2}(MDM).
 \end{aligned}$$ All these quadratic forms are taken on their supports; no invertibility of $M$ is required.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
###### The cubic trace.

<a id="eq:trace-Q"></a>
Choose an orthonormal eigenbasis $(e_i)$ of $M$, write the real eigenvalues as $m_i$, and arrange the indices so that $q_i:=|m_i|$ is nonincreasing. Equal values may be ordered arbitrarily. Write $D_{ij}$ for the entries of $D$ in this basis. The spectral formula for $b_{M^2}$ gives $$\label{eq:trace-Q}
 \mathcal E\ge\frac12Q,
 \qquad
 Q:=\sum_{i,j:q_i+q_j>0}
 \frac{q_i^2q_j^2}{q_i+q_j}|D_{ij}|^2.$$ If either eigenvalue is zero, its summand is zero; pairs of zero eigenvalues are omitted. Define $$A_i=q_i^3|D_{ii}|^2,\qquad
 E_i=q_i\sum_{j>i}q_j^2|D_{ij}|^2,
 \qquad A=\sum_iA_i,\quad E=\sum_iE_i.$$ Hermitian symmetry and the ordering of the $q_i$ imply <a id="eq:trace-AE"></a>$$\label{eq:trace-AE}
 \begin{aligned}
 Q
 &=\frac A2+
 2\sum_{\substack{i<j\\q_i+q_j>0}}
 \frac{q_i^2q_j^2}{q_i+q_j}|D_{ij}|^2
 \ge\frac A2+E.
 \end{aligned}$$ Indeed, for $i<j$ we have $q_j\le q_i$, so $2q_i/(q_i+q_j)\ge1$ whenever the denominator is positive.

We now bound $$\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}((DM)^3)
 =\sum_{i,j,k}m_im_jm_kD_{ij}D_{jk}D_{ki}$$ by grouping the triples according to how often their smallest index occurs. This is the index of a largest absolute eigenvalue in the triple.

If the smallest index $i$ occurs once, its three cyclic positions give $$3m_i\sum_{j,k>i}m_jm_kD_{ij}D_{jk}D_{ki}.$$ Here $j=k$ is allowed. For the vector $x=(m_jD_{ji})_{j>i}$, the inner sum is $x^*D_{>i}x$, where $D_{>i}$ is the compression of $D$ to the indices greater than $i$. Since $\lVert D_{>i}\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\le D_*$, the modulus of this contribution is at most $3D_*E_i$. This argument retains the signs of the $m_j$ and uses $D_{ji}=\overline{D_{ij}}$.

If the smallest index $i$ occurs twice, the contribution is $$3m_i^2D_{ii}\sum_{k>i}m_k|D_{ik}|^2.$$ Cauchy–Schwarz and $\sum_k|D_{ik}|^2=\lVert De_i\rVert^2\le D_*^2$ give $$\left|\sum_{k>i}m_k|D_{ik}|^2\right|
 \le D_*\left(\sum_{k>i}q_k^2|D_{ik}|^2\right)^{1/2}.$$ Its contribution therefore has modulus at most $3D_*\sqrt{A_iE_i}$. This also holds when $q_i=0$, without any division by $q_i$.

<a id="eq:trace-cubic"></a>
Finally, when all three indices equal $i$, the modulus is at most $D_*A_i$. These three cases exhaust all triples, including repeated indices. Summing them and applying Cauchy–Schwarz once more, we obtain $$\label{eq:trace-cubic}
 \begin{aligned}
 |\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}((DM)^3)|
 &\le D_*\bigl(A+3E+3\sqrt{AE}\bigr)\\
 &\le D_*\left(\frac52A+\frac92E\right)
 \le 5D_*\left(\frac A2+E\right)
 \le5D_*Q.
 \end{aligned}$$

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
###### Recovering the complex cyclic product.

We have bounded the cubic trace by the star costs. It remains to extract the modulus of the desired triangular trace. Put $$\tau=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(D_0W_0D_1W_1D_2W_2).$$ Because $M$ has zero diagonal blocks, a nonzero closed three-step block product must visit all three vertices. The three starting points in one orientation each give $\tau$, by cyclicity of the trace. Reversing the orientation gives $\overline\tau$, since the $D_v$ are Hermitian. Hence $$\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}((DM)^3)=6\operatorname{Re}\tau.$$ If $\tau\ne0$, choose $z\in\mathbb C$ with $|z|=1$ and $z\tau=|\tau|$, and replace $W_0$ by $zW_0$. The row stars change by $$\begin{aligned}
 R_0&\longmapsto
 R_0\mathop{\mathrm{diag}}(zI_{H_1},I_{H_2}),\\
 R_1&\longmapsto
 R_1\mathop{\mathrm{diag}}(I_{H_2},\overline zI_{H_0}),
 \end{aligned}$$ while $R_2$ is unchanged. Each displayed right multiplier is unitary and conjugates the corresponding Gram and inserted Gram by the same unitary. Thus $\mathcal E$ is unchanged, as is $D_*$. Applying <a href="#eq:trace-Q" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:trace-Q">[eq:trace-Q]</a> and <a href="#eq:trace-cubic" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:trace-cubic">[eq:trace-cubic]</a> to the modified matrices, with $Q$ computed for the new $M$, gives $$6|\tau|\le5D_*Q\le10D_*\mathcal E,$$ which is <a href="#eq:mixed-trace" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mixed-trace">[eq:mixed-trace]</a>. The case $\tau=0$ is immediate. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:heat"></a>
### Gaussian heat dissipation

We now weight the three edge matrices by Gaussians. For the variance rates chosen below, the sum of the star energies decreases after integration over a plane of centers. This decrease controls the matrix derivatives that will produce the odd kernel. The estimate must be independent of the number of matrix entries; this uniformity will allow us to pass from finite matrices to functions.

Continuous Gaussian telescoping for entangled forms appears in Durcik (Durcik 2015, Lemma 3 and Section 3) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[Durcik2015](#ref-Durcik2015)]. Here we use the Hessian of a matrix energy and compare its costs at neighboring vertices.

Fix $h>0$, an integer $N\ge0$, and nodes $u_i=ih$, $-N\le i\le N$. At vertex $v\in\mathbb Z/3\mathbb Z$ take $H_v=\mathbb C^{2N+1}$, and prescribe arbitrary complex edge values $A_v(i,j)$. For centers $p_v\in\mathbb R$ and variances $\sigma_v>0$, define $$\begin{gathered}
 g_\sigma(t)=(2\pi\sigma)^{-1/2}e^{-t^2/(2\sigma)},\qquad
 w_v(i)=h g_{\sigma_v}(u_i-p_v),\\
 W_v(i,j)=A_v(i,j)\sqrt{w_v(i)w_{v+1}(j)}.
\end{gathered}$$ Use the stars $R_v=[W_v\ W_{v-1}^*]$ and Grams $T_v=R_v^*R_v$, $S_v=R_vR_v^*$ from Section <a href="#sec:trace" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:trace">3</a>, and set $$e_v=\Phi(T_v)=\Phi(S_v).$$ Define the diagonal score and its insertions into the incident stars by $$\begin{aligned}
 L_w&=\mathop{\mathrm{diag}}_i\frac{u_i-p_w}{\sigma_w},
 &U_v&=R_v^*L_vR_v,\\
 U_{v,v+1}&=W_vL_{v+1}W_v^*,
 &U_{v,v-1}&=W_{v-1}^*L_{v-1}W_{v-1}.
\end{aligned}$$ Define their quadratic costs and the mixed term by <a id="eq:heat-costs"></a>$$\label{eq:heat-costs}
 V_v=b_{T_v}(U_v),\qquad
 Y_{v,w}=b_{S_v}(U_{v,w})\quad(w\ne v),\qquad
 Z_v=\mathcal B_{S_v}(U_{v,v+1},U_{v,v-1}).$$ Every insertion is supported on the Gram used in its definition: an inserted Gram $A^*BA$ annihilates $\ker A$ on both sides.

<a id="eq:heat-plane-measure"></a>
For $d\in\mathbb R$, let $\Pi_d=\{p_0+p_1+p_2=d\}$, with the measure $$\label{eq:heat-plane-measure}
 \int_{\Pi_d}H\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\pi_d
 =\int_{\mathbb R^2}H(p_0,p_1,d-p_0-p_1)\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm dp_0\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm dp_1.$$ Any other pair of free coordinates gives the same measure, since the change of coordinates has absolute determinant one. Put $\lambda=11/10$.

We also write $$\mathcal S(A)=\sum_{v=0}^2\sum_{i,j=-N}^Nh^2|A_v(i,j)|^3.$$ Our goal in this section is the fixed-array inequality $$-\partial_sJ(d,s)\ge c_*\int_{\Pi_d}\sum_vV_v\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\pi_d,
 \qquad J(d,s)=\int_{\Pi_d}\sum_ve_v\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\pi_d,$$ when the variances are $\sigma_i=\alpha_i s$ and $\boldsymbol\alpha$ is a permutation of $(1,\lambda,\lambda)$. The odd-window estimate in Section <a href="#sec:maximal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:maximal">6</a> uses wider Gaussian weights for uniformly bounded diagonal factors, but compares each score cost after narrowing only its own vertex. The fixed enlargement $\lambda=11/10$ retains strict dissipation for all three comparisons. Lemma <a href="#lem:plane-dissipation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:plane-dissipation">9</a> gives an absolute $c_*>0$; Lemma <a href="#lem:initial-energy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:initial-energy">10</a> bounds $J$ by $C\mathcal S(A)$ at scales $s\ge h^2$. Later, when entries change at prescribed scales, the same differential inequality will apply between successive changes.

We first differentiate the star energies, choosing the Gram orientation whose support stays fixed. We then integrate over the center plane and compare neighboring stars. These two steps produce the strict decrease that controls the score costs.

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### Differentiating the star energies

<a id="stmt-lem-star-smoothness"></a>
<div id="lem:star-smoothness" class="lemma">

**Lemma 6**. *For fixed finite edge arrays, each $e_v$ is smooth in all centers and positive variances. If only the parameters at vertex $v$ vary, the support of $T_v$ is fixed. If the parameters at either or both other vertices vary while those at $v$ are fixed, the support of $S_v$ is fixed.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-star-smoothness"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-star-smoothness</summary>

*Proof.* The weighted star has the form $R_v=D_{\rm row}R_v^0D_{\rm col}$, where $R_v^0=[A_v\ A_{v-1}^*]$ is fixed and both diagonal multipliers are strictly positive; the column multiplier has one block for each neighbor. Invertible row multiplication preserves the kernel, and invertible column multiplication preserves the range. This proves the two support assertions, since $$\ker R_v=\ker(R_v^0D_{\rm col}),\qquad
 \operatorname{ran}R_v=D_{\rm row}\operatorname{ran}R_v^0$$ when the respective other multiplier is held fixed.

For joint smoothness, the rank-zero case is immediate. Otherwise factor $R_v^0=XY^*$ with $X,Y$ of full column rank. Write $X'=D_{\rm row}X$, $Y'=D_{\rm col}Y$ and $P'=X'^*X'$, $Q'=Y'^*Y'$. These last two matrices are positive definite and depend smoothly on the parameters. The nonzero eigenvalues of $R_vR_v^*=X'Q'X'^*$ are those of $Q'^{1/2}P'Q'^{1/2}$, by comparing $AA^*$ and $A^*A$ for $A=X'Q'^{1/2}$. Therefore $$e_v=\Phi(Q'^{1/2}P'Q'^{1/2}),$$ which is smooth on the positive definite cone. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-heat-identities"></a>
<div id="lem:heat-identities" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:heat-identity"></a>
<a id="eq:heat-mixed"></a>
**Lemma 7**. *Fix a positive vector $\boldsymbol\alpha$ and set $\sigma_i=\alpha_i s$. For all centers and all $s>0$, $$\begin{aligned}
 2\partial_s e_v
 &=\sum_i\alpha_i\partial_{p_i}^2e_v
       -\alpha_vV_v-\sum_{w\ne v}\alpha_wY_{v,w},
       \label{eq:heat-identity}\\
 \partial_{p_{v+1}}\partial_{p_{v-1}}e_v&=Z_v.
       \label{eq:heat-mixed}
\end{aligned}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-heat-identities"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-heat-identities</summary>

*Proof.* For $w=h g_\sigma(u-p)$ and $L=(u-p)/\sigma$, $$\partial_pw=Lw,\qquad
 \partial_p^2w=(L^2-\sigma^{-1})w,\qquad
 \partial_\sigma w=\tfrac12\partial_p^2w.$$ With the columns fixed, $T_v$ is linear in the row density, so $$\partial_{p_v}T_v=U_v,\qquad
 \partial_{\sigma_v}T_v=\tfrac12\partial_{p_v}^2T_v.$$ Its support is fixed by Lemma <a href="#lem:star-smoothness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:star-smoothness">6</a>. The chain rule and Lemma <a href="#lem:hessian" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:hessian">2</a> thus give $$\partial_{\sigma_v}e_v
 =\tfrac12\partial_{p_v}^2e_v
    -\tfrac12\mathcal B_{T_v}(U_v,U_v)
 =\tfrac12\partial_{p_v}^2e_v-\tfrac12V_v.$$ For a column vertex $w\ne v$, use instead $S_v$, which is linear in that column density and has fixed support. This gives $$\partial_{p_w}S_v=U_{v,w},\qquad
 \partial_{\sigma_w}e_v=\tfrac12\partial_{p_w}^2e_v-\tfrac12Y_{v,w}.$$ Summing the three variance derivatives with coefficients $\alpha_i$ proves <a href="#eq:heat-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:heat-identity">[eq:heat-identity]</a>.

The two summands in $S_v=W_vW_v^*+W_{v-1}^*W_{v-1}$ depend on different column densities. Its mixed derivative in the two column centers is therefore zero. Only the Hessian term remains in the mixed derivative of $e_v$, giving <a href="#eq:heat-mixed" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:heat-mixed">[eq:heat-mixed]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### Comparing neighbors and integrating centers

<a id="stmt-lem-neighbors"></a>
<div id="lem:neighbors" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:heat-neighbors"></a>
**Lemma 8**. *For every choice of the weighted matrices and every vertex $w$, $$\label{eq:heat-neighbors}
 Y_{w+1,w}+Y_{w-1,w}\le\sqrt2\,V_w.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-neighbors"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-neighbors</summary>

*Proof.* Abbreviate $A=W_w$, $B=W_{w-1}^*$, and $L=L_w$. The inequality $\lVert Az+Bz'\rVert^2\le2\lVert Az\rVert^2+2\lVert Bz'\rVert^2$ implies $$T_w\le2Q,\qquad Q=\mathop{\mathrm{diag}}(A^*A,B^*B).$$ Since $U_w=[A\ B]^*L[A\ B]$ is supported on $T_w$, Lemma <a href="#lem:order" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:order">3</a> gives $b_{T_w}(U_w)\ge2^{-1/2}b_Q(U_w)$. In a block-preserving eigenbasis of $Q$, the formula for $b_Q$ is a sum of nonnegative squared entries. Discarding the off-diagonal blocks yields $$b_Q(U_w)\ge b_{A^*A}(A^*LA)+b_{B^*B}(B^*LB).$$ Here $A^*A\le S_{w+1}$ and $B^*B\le S_{w-1}$, and the respective insertions are $U_{w+1,w}$ and $U_{w-1,w}$. Each is supported on its smaller edge Gram. Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:order" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:order">3</a> again, now with comparison factor one, to obtain <a href="#eq:heat-neighbors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:heat-neighbors">[eq:heat-neighbors]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-plane-dissipation"></a>
<div id="lem:plane-dissipation" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:heat-plane-dissipation"></a>
<a id="eq:heat-strict"></a>
**Lemma 9**. *Fix finite edge arrays and a positive vector $\boldsymbol\alpha$, and evaluate the energies at $\sigma_i=\alpha_i s$. Set $$J_v(d,s)=\int_{\Pi_d}e_v\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\pi_d,
 \qquad J(d,s)=\sum_vJ_v(d,s),
 \qquad \Sigma_\alpha=\sum_i\alpha_i.$$ These integrals are finite, continuously differentiable in $s>0$, and twice continuously differentiable in $d$. Moreover, $$\label{eq:heat-plane-dissipation}
 2\partial_sJ(d,s)\le\int_{\Pi_d}\sum_w
 \left[-\alpha_w+
 \sqrt2\max\{0,\Sigma_\alpha/2-\alpha_w\}\right]V_w\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\pi_d.$$ In particular, for each permutation of $(1,\lambda,\lambda)$, $$\label{eq:heat-strict}
 -\partial_sJ(d,s)\ge c_*\int_{\Pi_d}\sum_wV_w\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\pi_d,
 \qquad
 c_* = \frac12\min\{1-\tfrac35\sqrt2,\tfrac{11}{10}-\tfrac12\sqrt2\}>0.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-plane-dissipation"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-plane-dissipation</summary>

*Proof.* We first justify differentiation at the fixed mesh. On compact positive $s$-intervals the nodes are bounded, the scores grow at most linearly in the centers, and the first and second density ratios grow at most quadratically. Lemma <a href="#lem:insertion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:insertion">4</a> gives $$V_v\le\tfrac34\lVert L_v\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}^2e_v,
 \qquad Y_{v,w}\le\tfrac34\lVert L_w\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}^2e_v.$$ For the second inequality, pad $L_w$ by zero on the other column block, obtaining $B_w$ with $U_{v,w}=R_vB_wR_v^*$, and apply the lemma to $R_v^*$. Also $2|Z_v|\le Y_{v,v+1}+Y_{v,v-1}$. For a Hermitian matrix $B$, $$|\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(\sqrt{T_v}R_v^*BR_v)|\le\lVert B\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}e_v,$$ since $R_v\sqrt{T_v}R_v^*$ is positive with trace $e_v$. The corresponding column estimate follows in the same way with $S_v$ and a padded column insertion. Together with the density-ratio formulas in Lemma <a href="#lem:heat-identities" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:heat-identities">7</a>, these bounds control every first center derivative, pure second center derivative, mixed column derivative, and first $s$-derivative by a polynomial in the centers times $e_v$.

<a id="eq:heat-energy-bound"></a>
To dominate the energy itself, use $$\label{eq:heat-energy-bound}
 e_v\le(\lVert W_v\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^2+
            \lVert W_{v-1}\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^2)^{3/2}
 \le\sqrt2(\lVert W_v\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^3+
                  \lVert W_{v-1}\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^3).$$ Each edge norm cubed is bounded by a Gaussian in its two endpoint centers, locally uniformly for $s>0$. Indeed, the finite set of nodes is bounded and $(u-p)^2\ge p^2/2-u^2$. On $\Pi_d$ with $|d|\le D$, any pair of centers satisfies $$p_0^2+p_1^2+p_2^2\le4(p_i^2+p_j^2)+3D^2.$$ Thus an endpoint-pair Gaussian, multiplied by any of the preceding polynomials, has an integrable majorant in any fixed plane chart, locally uniformly in $(d,s)$. This proves all needed differentiation statements by dominated convergence. The constants in this justification may depend on the finite mesh and the compact parameter intervals; they will not enter the uniform estimate.

We can now differentiate the plane integrals. It remains to express their second derivatives in $d$ using both pure and mixed center derivatives, then apply Lemma <a href="#lem:neighbors" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:neighbors">8</a> to the resulting costs.

<a id="eq:heat-plane-mixed"></a>
Choosing $p_i$ as the dependent coordinate in <a href="#eq:heat-plane-measure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:heat-plane-measure">[eq:heat-plane-measure]</a> gives $$\partial_d^2J_v=\int_{\Pi_d}\partial_{p_i}^2e_v\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\pi_d
 \qquad\text{for every }i.$$ For the column indices $j=v+1$, $k=v-1$, first choose $p_j$ dependent to write $\partial_dJ_v=\int_{\Pi_d}\partial_{p_j}e_v\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\pi_d$. Reparametrize this last integral with $p_k$ dependent, then differentiate again. It follows from <a href="#eq:heat-mixed" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:heat-mixed">[eq:heat-mixed]</a> that $$\label{eq:heat-plane-mixed}
 \partial_d^2J_v
 =\int_{\Pi_d}\partial_{p_k}\partial_{p_j}e_v\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\pi_d
 =\int_{\Pi_d}Z_v\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\pi_d.$$ These are identities of integrated derivatives; no pointwise equality between pure and mixed derivatives is required.

Integrating <a href="#eq:heat-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:heat-identity">[eq:heat-identity]</a> and using <a href="#eq:heat-plane-mixed" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:heat-plane-mixed">[eq:heat-plane-mixed]</a>, we obtain $$2\partial_sJ_v=\int_{\Pi_d}
 \left[\Sigma_\alpha Z_v-\alpha_vV_v
                   -\sum_{w\ne v}\alpha_wY_{v,w}\right]\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\pi_d.$$ Cauchy–Schwarz gives $2Z_v\le Y_{v,v+1}+Y_{v,v-1}$. Sum over $v$ and collect the two neighbor costs carrying the score at $w$. Their common coefficient is $\Sigma_\alpha/2-\alpha_w$. If it is negative, discard those terms; otherwise apply Lemma <a href="#lem:neighbors" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:neighbors">8</a>. This proves <a href="#eq:heat-plane-dissipation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:heat-plane-dissipation">[eq:heat-plane-dissipation]</a>.

For the indicated permutations, $\Sigma_\alpha=16/5$. The two possible coefficients in <a href="#eq:heat-plane-dissipation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:heat-plane-dissipation">[eq:heat-plane-dissipation]</a> are $-1+(3/5)\sqrt2$ and $-11/10+(1/2)\sqrt2$, both strictly negative. Dividing by two gives <a href="#eq:heat-strict" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:heat-strict">[eq:heat-strict]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### The initial energy

The previous lemma controls the accumulated costs by the energy lost along the heat flow. We now bound the energy itself at scales no smaller than the mesh. This estimate will also apply immediately after entries are switched on or off, since it requires only a bound on their absolute values.

<a id="stmt-lem-initial-energy"></a>
<div id="lem:initial-energy" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:initial-energy"></a>
**Lemma 10** (Initial energy). *Let $h>0$, let $N\ge0$ be an integer, and let $A_v(i,j)$ be the finite complex arrays above. For any $d\in\mathbb R$ and variances $\sigma_v\ge h^2$, $$\label{eq:initial-energy}
 \int_{\Pi_d}\sum_ve_v\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\pi_d
 \le C_0\mathcal S(A),\qquad
 C_0=2\sqrt2\left(1+\sqrt{2/\pi}\right).$$ The same bound holds if any entries are replaced by smaller entries in absolute value before forming the weighted matrices.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-initial-energy"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-initial-energy</summary>

*Proof.* For every center $p$ and variance $\sigma>0$, <a id="eq:heat-mesh-mass"></a>$$\label{eq:heat-mesh-mass}
 \sum_{i=-N}^Nh g_\sigma(u_i-p)
 \le1+\frac{2h}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma}}.$$ To see this, compare a grid point $u_i\ge p+h$ with the integral over its preceding interval of length $h$, where the Gaussian is at least its value at $u_i$. For $u_i\le p-h$, use the succeeding interval. These intervals are disjoint, and the at most two remaining grid points each contribute at most $h\sup g_\sigma$.

Since each $\sigma_v\ge h^2$, every density has total mass at most $M=1+\sqrt{2/\pi}$. Weighted Hölder gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \lVert W_v\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^3
 &=\left(\sum_{i,j}|A_v(i,j)|^2w_v(i)w_{v+1}(j)\right)^{3/2}\\
 &\le M\sum_{i,j}|A_v(i,j)|^3w_v(i)w_{v+1}(j).
\end{aligned}$$ Integrate with $p_v,p_{v+1}$ as free coordinates on $\Pi_d$. Each density has integral $h$, so $$\int_{\Pi_d}\lVert W_v\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^3\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\pi_d
 \le M\sum_{i,j}h^2|A_v(i,j)|^3.$$ Each edge occurs in exactly two stars. Equation <a href="#eq:heat-energy-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:heat-energy-bound">[eq:heat-energy-bound]</a> therefore proves <a href="#eq:initial-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:initial-energy">[eq:initial-energy]</a>. Replacing entries by smaller ones in absolute value only reduces the last upper bounds, proving the final assertion. ◻

</details>

For fixed arrays and a permutation $\boldsymbol\alpha$ of $(1,\lambda,\lambda)$, Lemmas <a href="#lem:plane-dissipation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:plane-dissipation">9</a> and <a href="#lem:initial-energy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:initial-energy">10</a> now give, whenever $h^2\le s_0<s_1$, $$\int_{s_0}^{s_1}\int_{\Pi_0}\sum_vV_v\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\pi_0\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm ds
 \le\frac{J(0,s_0)-J(0,s_1)}{c_*}
 \le\frac{C_0}{c_*}\mathcal S(A).$$ The first inequality retains the final energy. In the next section, we will add these inequalities over intervals with different masked arrays.

<a id="sec:jumps"></a>
### Output-dependent truncations and energy jumps

We now allow each entry of one edge array to have its own truncation interval. Between endpoint scales, the heat estimate from Section <a href="#sec:heat" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:heat">4</a> still applies. The new task is to bound the total increase in energy when entries switch on or off. We will in fact bound the sum of the absolute energy jumps. The estimate depends on the original arrays alone, because every entry switches at most twice. We first establish two inputs to this bound: estimates for Gaussian averages that are uniform in the scale, and a formula for changes in the matrix energy that remains valid when the rank changes.

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
#### Maximal averages on a line

For a locally integrable function on $\mathbb R$, and a sequence on $\mathbb Z$, respectively, define the centered maximal functions $$M_{\mathbb R}f(x)=\sup_{r>0}\frac1{2r}\int_{x-r}^{x+r}|f(t)|\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm dt,
 \qquad
 M_{\mathbb Z}f(n)=\sup_{r\in\mathbb Z_{\ge0}}
       \frac1{2r+1}\sum_{|m-n|\le r}|f(m)|.$$ Thus discrete averages include singletons. We use Lebesgue measure on $\mathbb R$ and counting measure on $\mathbb Z$. The estimates below are versions of the classical Hardy–Littlewood maximal inequalities (Hardy and Littlewood 1930, sec. IV) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[HardyLittlewood1930](#ref-HardyLittlewood1930)]; we include the centered covering argument and the Gaussian comparison with the constants needed here.

<a id="stmt-lem-line-maximal"></a>
<div id="lem:line-maximal" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:line-maximal-strong"></a>
<a id="eq:discrete-gaussian-maximal"></a>
**Lemma 11** (One-dimensional maximal estimates). *On either of these measure spaces, write $M$ for the corresponding centered maximal operator. For $f\in L^1$ and $\rho>0$, $$\mu\{Mf>\rho\}\le\frac3\rho\|f\|_1.$$ For every $1<q<\infty$ and $f\in L^q$, $$\label{eq:line-maximal-strong}
 \|Mf\|_q^q\le
 \frac{6q\,2^{q-1}}{q-1}\|f\|_q^q.$$ Moreover, there is an absolute constant $C_g$ such that, whenever $h>0$, $\sigma\ge h^2$ and $n\in\mathbb Z$, $$\label{eq:discrete-gaussian-maximal}
 \sum_{m\in\mathbb Z}h g_\sigma((m-n)h)|f(m)|
 \le C_g M_{\mathbb Z}f(n).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-line-maximal"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-line-maximal</summary>

*Proof.* For a finite collection of intervals, repeatedly choose an interval of largest radius and discard all intervals meeting it. The chosen intervals are disjoint. Every discarded interval has radius no larger than that of the interval which removed it and is contained in its triple-radius enlargement. This enlargement has three times the original length on $\mathbb R$. On $\mathbb Z$, its cardinality is $6r+1\le3(2r+1)$, including $r=0$.

For each point in a finite subset of $\{M_{\mathbb Z}f>\rho\}$, choose a witnessing interval whose average exceeds $\rho$. The preceding selection bounds the size of that subset by three times the sum of the cardinalities of the chosen intervals, and hence by $3\|f\|_1/\rho$. Taking the supremum over finite subsets proves the discrete weak estimate. On $\mathbb R$, fixed-radius averages of an $L^1$ function are continuous, so $\{M_{\mathbb R}f>\rho\}$ is open. Apply the same selection to a finite witnessing cover of any compact subset and then use inner regularity to obtain the continuous estimate.

For $f\in L^q$, split $f$ into its parts on $\{|f|\le\rho/2\}$ and $\{|f|>\rho/2\}$. The first part has maximal function at most $\rho/2$, and the second is integrable. The weak estimate gives $$\mu\{Mf>\rho\}
 \le\frac6\rho\int_{|f|>\rho/2}|f|\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\mu.$$ The distribution formula and Tonelli’s theorem now yield $$\begin{aligned}
 \|Mf\|_q^q
 &\le6q\int |f(x)|
          \int_0^{2|f(x)|}\rho^{q-2}\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\rho\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\mu(x)\\
 &=\frac{6q\,2^{q-1}}{q-1}\|f\|_q^q.
 \end{aligned}$$ These formulas are valid first with a bounded range of distribution parameters and then by monotone convergence; finiteness of $Mf$ is not assumed in advance. Both maximal functions are measurable: the discrete supremum is countable, and continuous averages are continuous in their radius, so positive rational radii suffice on $\mathbb R$.

Finally put $a=\sqrt\sigma/h\ge1$. In the sum in <a href="#eq:discrete-gaussian-maximal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:discrete-gaussian-maximal">[eq:discrete-gaussian-maximal]</a>, the kernel is $$h g_\sigma((m-n)h)
   =\frac1{\sqrt{2\pi}a}
       \exp\left(-\frac{(m-n)^2}{2a^2}\right).$$ Split the integers into $|m-n|\le a$ and the annuli $2^{j-1}a<|m-n|\le2^ja$, $j\ge1$. The sum of $|f|$ over $|m-n|\le2^ja$ is at most $3\cdot2^ja\,M_{\mathbb Z}f(n)$. Bounding the Gaussian by its maximum on each region proves the claim with, for example, $$C_g=\frac3{\sqrt{2\pi}}
 \left(1+\sum_{j\ge1}2^j e^{-4^{j-1}/2}\right)<\infty.$$ All constants are independent of the mesh and of the support of $f$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
#### Changing a matrix entry

At a truncation endpoint the rank of a star can change. We therefore need a first-derivative formula that does not require fixed support. For the sum of cubes of the singular values, this formula is a case of the general gradient formula for unitarily invariant matrix functions (Lewis 1995, Theorem 3.1) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[Lewis1995](#ref-Lewis1995)]. We give a direct regularization proof and then establish the entrywise bound used in the jump estimate.

<a id="stmt-lem-rank-change"></a>
<div id="lem:rank-change" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:rank-change-derivative"></a>
<a id="eq:entry-gradient"></a>
**Lemma 12** (A derivative through rank changes). *For finite rectangular complex matrices $R_-,R_+$ of the same size, put $R(z)=(1-z)R_-+zR_+$ and $D=R_+-R_-$. Then $$\label{eq:rank-change-derivative}
 \begin{aligned}
 &\Phi(R_+^*R_+)-\Phi(R_-^*R_-)\\
 &\quad=3\int_0^1\operatorname{Re}
   \langle R(z)\sqrt{R(z)^*R(z)},D\rangle_{\mathrm{HS}}\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm dz.
 \end{aligned}$$ For every rectangular matrix $R$ and every entry $(i,j)$, $$\label{eq:entry-gradient}
 |(R\sqrt{R^*R})_{ij}|
 \le\|\operatorname{row}_iR\|_2\,
       \|\operatorname{col}_jR\|_2.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-rank-change"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-rank-change</summary>

*Proof.* For $\eta>0$, differentiate $\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}((R(z)^*R(z)+\eta I)^{3/2})$ using Lemma <a href="#lem:hessian" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:hessian">2</a>. Its derivative is $$3\operatorname{Re}
 \langle R(z)\sqrt{R(z)^*R(z)+\eta I},D\rangle_{\mathrm{HS}}.$$ Spectral calculus gives $$\|\sqrt{R^*R+\eta I}-\sqrt{R^*R}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le\sqrt\eta.$$ Since $R(z)$ stays in a bounded set, the derivatives converge uniformly in $z$, and the endpoint energies converge. Integrating the regularized identity before letting $\eta\downarrow0$ proves <a href="#eq:rank-change-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rank-change-derivative">[eq:rank-change-derivative]</a>.

Cauchy–Schwarz in the matrix-product formula bounds the left side of <a href="#eq:entry-gradient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:entry-gradient">[eq:entry-gradient]</a> by the norm of row $i$ of $R$ times the norm of column $j$ of $\sqrt{R^*R}$. The squared norm of that column is $(R^*R)_{jj}$, which is also the squared norm of column $j$ of $R$. This proves <a href="#eq:entry-gradient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:entry-gradient">[eq:entry-gradient]</a>, including zero columns and singular matrices. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### The total jump bound

Return to the finite arrays and Gaussian weights of Section <a href="#sec:heat" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:heat">4</a>, and write $\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\pi=\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\pi_0$ on $\Pi_0$. Independently for every pair $(i,j)$, choose finite endpoints $$h\le\varepsilon_{ij}<\rho_{ij},
 \qquad
 A_0^s(i,j)=A_0(i,j)
       \mathbf1_{\{\varepsilon_{ij}^2<s<\rho_{ij}^2\}}.$$ The chosen endpoints are fixed independently of the Gaussian centers. Keep $A_1,A_2$ fixed and form the weighted matrices and costs using $A_0^s$ in place of $A_0$. The arrays are complex; any phase at an output pair may be included in the fixed value $A_0(i,j)$. Set $$s_- =\min_{i,j}\varepsilon_{ij}^2,
 \qquad s_+=\max_{i,j}\rho_{ij}^2.$$

<a id="stmt-prop-masked-dissipation"></a>
<div id="prop:masked-dissipation" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:masked-dissipation"></a>
**Proposition 13** (Dissipation with output-dependent endpoints). *For every $h>0$, $N\ge0$, complex arrays $A_v$ on $\{-N,\ldots,N\}^2$, and endpoint choices as above, let $\alpha$ be any permutation of $(1,11/10,11/10)$ and use variances $\sigma_v=\alpha_vs$. The corresponding masked costs satisfy $$\label{eq:masked-dissipation}
 \int_{s_-}^{s_+}\int_{\Pi_0}
       \sum_{v=0}^2 V_v(p,s)\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\pi\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm ds
 \le C\mathcal S(A).$$ The constant is independent of the arrays, the mesh, the endpoints, and the number of distinct endpoint values.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-masked-dissipation"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-masked-dissipation</summary>

*Proof.* Write $J(s)=\int_{\Pi_0}\sum_ve_v(p,s)\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\pi$ for the masked energy, and put $\Delta J(\tau)=J(\tau+)-J(\tau-)$ at an endpoint. We first estimate its jump at a single scale, then sum those estimates and telescope the heat inequality.

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
###### The two stars at a jump.

At an endpoint scale $\tau$, hold the weights at $s=\tau$ and let $$\delta A(i,j)=A_0^{\tau+}(i,j)-A_0^{\tau-}(i,j).$$ The superscripts denote one-sided masks. Interpolate all changed entries at once between these two arrays. Each interpolated entry has absolute value at most $|A_0(i,j)|$, even when some entries open and others close at the same scale.

<a id="eq:jump-entry-bound"></a>
For fixed $i,j,p$, define nonnegative numbers using the original, unmasked arrays: <a id="eq:jump-slice-quantities"></a>$$\label{eq:jump-slice-quantities}
 \begin{aligned}
 X^2&=\sum_m|A_0(i,m)|^2w_1(m),&
 Y^2&=\sum_m|A_0(m,j)|^2w_0(m),\\
 H^2&=\sum_m|A_2(m,i)|^2w_2(m),&
 K^2&=\sum_m|A_1(j,m)|^2w_2(m).
 \end{aligned}$$ In $R_0=[W_0\;W_2^*]$, a changing entry has row norm at most $\sqrt{w_0(i)}\sqrt{X^2+H^2}$, column norm at most $\sqrt{w_1(j)}Y$, and derivative modulus $\sqrt{w_0(i)w_1(j)}|\delta A(i,j)|$. In $R_1=[W_1\;W_0^*]$, its adjoint entry has row norm at most $\sqrt{w_1(j)}\sqrt{Y^2+K^2}$ and column norm at most $\sqrt{w_0(i)}X$, with the same derivative modulus. Star $R_2=[W_2\;W_1^*]$ is unchanged. Lemma <a href="#lem:rank-change" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:rank-change">12</a> and $\sqrt{x^2+y^2}\le x+y$ therefore give $$\label{eq:jump-entry-bound}
 \begin{aligned}
 |\Delta e_0|+|\Delta e_1|
 \le3\sum_{i,j}|\delta A(i,j)|w_0(i)w_1(j)
       \bigl[(X+H)Y+(Y+K)X\bigr].
 \end{aligned}$$ This estimate requires no assumption on the ranks during interpolation.

<a id="pm-h-19"></a>
###### A probability measure on the center plane.

<a id="eq:jump-gaussian-expectations"></a>
For each fixed $i,j$, the measure $$\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\nu_{ij}=h^{-2}w_0(i)w_1(j)\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\pi$$ is a probability measure. Indeed, in the free coordinates $(p_0,p_1)$, its density is $g_{\sigma_0}(u_i-p_0)g_{\sigma_1}(u_j-p_1)$. Thus $p_0,p_1$ are independent Gaussian variables with means $u_i,u_j$ and variances $\sigma_0,\sigma_1$, while $p_2=-p_0-p_1$. Adding Gaussian variances gives $$\label{eq:jump-gaussian-expectations}
 \begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E_{\nu_{ij}}w_0(m)
     &=h g_{2\sigma_0}(u_m-u_i),\\
 \mathbb E_{\nu_{ij}}w_1(m)
     &=h g_{2\sigma_1}(u_m-u_j),\\
 \mathbb E_{\nu_{ij}}w_2(m)
     &=h g_{\sigma_0+\sigma_1+\sigma_2}(u_m+u_i+u_j).
 \end{aligned}$$ For the last identity, $p_0+p_1$ has mean $u_i+u_j$ and variance $\sigma_0+\sigma_1$, and averaging $g_{\sigma_2}(u_m+p_0+p_1)$ adds the remaining variance. Each identity also follows directly by completing the square in its Gaussian integral.

Extend the arrays by zero to $\mathbb Z^2$ and define <a id="eq:jump-starred-majorants"></a>$$\label{eq:jump-starred-majorants}
 \begin{aligned}
 X_*^2(i,j)&=M_{\mathbb Z}(|A_0(i,\cdot)|^2)(j),\\
 Y_*^2(i,j)&=M_{\mathbb Z}(|A_0(\cdot,j)|^2)(i),\\
 H_*^2(i,j)&=M_{\mathbb Z}(|A_2(\cdot,i)|^2)(-i-j),\\
 K_*^2(i,j)&=M_{\mathbb Z}(|A_1(j,\cdot)|^2)(-i-j).
 \end{aligned}$$ Every variance in <a href="#eq:jump-gaussian-expectations" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:jump-gaussian-expectations">[eq:jump-gaussian-expectations]</a> is at least $h^2$. Lemma <a href="#lem:line-maximal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:line-maximal">11</a> consequently bounds $\mathbb E X^2,\mathbb E Y^2,\mathbb E H^2,\mathbb E K^2$ by $C_g$ times the corresponding starred squares. Cauchy–Schwarz under the probability $\nu_{ij}$ gives, in particular, $$\mathbb E(XY)\le C_gX_*Y_*,\qquad
 \mathbb E(HY)\le C_gH_*Y_*,\qquad
 \mathbb E(KX)\le C_gK_*X_*.$$ No independence of these four random variables is needed. The starred quantities depend only on the original arrays and the output indices, not on the scale $\tau$.

<a id="pm-h-20"></a>
###### Summing the jumps.

<a id="eq:total-jump-majorant"></a>
Integrating <a href="#eq:jump-entry-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:jump-entry-bound">[eq:jump-entry-bound]</a> over the centers and using $w_0(i)w_1(j)\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\pi=h^2\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\nu_{ij}$ yields $$|\Delta J(\tau)|\le3C_g\sum_{i,j}h^2|\delta A(i,j)|
       (2X_*Y_*+H_*Y_*+K_*X_*).$$ Every entry opens once and closes once. Therefore its total absolute change, summed over any subset of endpoint scales, is at most $2|A_0(i,j)|$. We obtain $$\label{eq:total-jump-majorant}
 \sum_\tau|\Delta J(\tau)|
 \le6C_g\sum_{i,j}h^2|A_0(i,j)|
       (2X_*Y_*+H_*Y_*+K_*X_*).$$ The sum may include all endpoints or just the interior ones.

<a id="eq:jump-slice-norms"></a>
To bound this expression, set $$n_v=\left(\sum_{i,j}h^2|A_v(i,j)|^3\right)^{1/3},
 \qquad \|f\|_{3,h}^3=\sum_{i,j\in\mathbb Z}h^2|f(i,j)|^3.$$ The $q=3/2$ case of <a href="#eq:line-maximal-strong" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:line-maximal-strong">[eq:line-maximal-strong]</a>, applied on slices, gives $$\label{eq:jump-slice-norms}
 \|X_*\|_{3,h},\ \|Y_*\|_{3,h}\le Cn_0,
 \qquad \|H_*\|_{3,h}\le Cn_2,
 \qquad \|K_*\|_{3,h}\le Cn_1.$$ For $H_*$, fix $i$ and change the summation index from $j$ to $-i-j$; this is a bijection of $\mathbb Z$. For $K_*$, fix $j$ and change $i$ to $-i-j$. Thus both estimates are ordinary slice estimates, with no multiplicity or mesh factor. Restricting the sums back to the original finite square can only decrease them.

<a id="eq:total-jump-bound"></a>
Hölder’s inequality in <a href="#eq:total-jump-majorant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:total-jump-majorant">[eq:total-jump-majorant]</a>, followed by <a href="#eq:jump-slice-norms" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:jump-slice-norms">[eq:jump-slice-norms]</a>, proves $$\label{eq:total-jump-bound}
 \sum_\tau|\Delta J(\tau)|
 \le C(2n_0^3+n_0^2n_2+n_0^2n_1)
 \le C\mathcal S(A).$$ The last step uses $a^2b\le(2a^3+b^3)/3$. In particular, the estimate has no dependence on how many distinct scales occur.

<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
###### Telescoping the heat estimate.

List the distinct squared endpoints as $t_0=s_-<t_1<\cdots<t_m=s_+$. On every open interval $(t_k,t_{k+1})$ the array is fixed, so Lemma <a href="#lem:plane-dissipation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:plane-dissipation">9</a> applies. Its fixed-array continuity and Gaussian domination also justify the one-sided endpoint limits. Integrating on these intervals and summing gives $$\begin{aligned}
 c_*\int_{s_-}^{s_+}\int_{\Pi_0}\sum_vV_v\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\pi\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm ds
 &\le\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\bigl[J(t_k+)-J(t_{k+1}-)\bigr]\\
 &=J(s_-+)-J(s_+-)
       +\sum_{k=1}^{m-1}\bigl[J(t_k+)-J(t_k-)\bigr]\\
 &\le J(s_-+)+\sum_{k=1}^{m-1}|\Delta J(t_k)|.
\end{aligned}$$ Here the terminal energy was discarded by nonnegativity. The initial right-hand mask has entries bounded by those of the original array, and all its variances are at least $h^2$. Lemma <a href="#lem:initial-energy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:initial-energy">10</a> therefore gives $J(s_-+)\le C\mathcal S(A)$. Together with <a href="#eq:total-jump-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:total-jump-bound">[eq:total-jump-bound]</a>, this proves <a href="#eq:masked-dissipation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:masked-dissipation">[eq:masked-dissipation]</a>. The initial opening is accounted for by the right-hand initial energy; a final closing jump is unnecessary. Simultaneous endpoints have already been combined in the list of distinct scales, and their values at the single endpoint do not affect the scale integral. ◻

</details>

Figure <a href="#fig:mask-energy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:mask-energy">2</a> illustrates the masks and the energy accounting.

<figure id="fig:mask-energy">

<figcaption>Three selected output pairs and a schematic integrated energy, not an exact heat-flow solution. The entry $A_{0}^{s}(i,j)$ is retained only on $(ε_{ij}^{2},ρ_{ij}^{2})$. Between successive event scales, $J(s)$ is nonincreasing by the fixed-array dissipation inequality. Dashed arrows connect its one-sided limits at events; the values at the events themselves are not shown. Jumps may have either sign, and endpoints may coincide, as illustrated by $ρ_{i1}j_{1}^{2} = ε_{i3}j_{3}^{2}$. The bound $∑_{τ}|ΔJ(τ)| ≤ C𝒮(A)$, together with the initial-energy bound, controls the accumulated dissipation independently of the number of event scales.</figcaption>
</figure>

```text
Two vertically aligned diagrams:
- Top diagram ("Output-dependent scale intervals"): Horizontal axis labeled $s$ with an arrow pointing right. Three rows labeled $(i_1, j_1)$, $(i_2, j_2)$, and $(i_3, j_3)$ showing intervals drawn in blue, each with an open circle at the left endpoint and a filled circle at the right endpoint. Endpoints are marked $\varepsilon_{i_1 j_1}^2$ and $\rho_{i_1 j_1}^2$ for the first pair; $\varepsilon_{i_2 j_2}^2$ and $\rho_{i_2 j_2}^2$ for the second pair; $\varepsilon_{i_3 j_3}^2$ and $\rho_{i_3 j_3}^2$ for the third pair. The point $\rho_{i_1 j_1}^2$ shares the same horizontal position as $\varepsilon_{i_3 j_3}^2$. Light vertical guide lines extend downward from all endpoints.
- Bottom diagram ("Integrated energy"): Horizontal axis labeled $s$ with an arrow pointing right, marked with endpoints $s_-$ and $s_+$. Vertical axis labeled $J(s)$. The function $J(s)$ is plotted as solid black curves that decrease within each interval between events. At event coordinates corresponding to interval endpoints above, red dashed vertical arrows connect one-sided limits: an upward jump at $\varepsilon_{i_2 j_2}^2$, a downward jump at $\rho_{i_1 j_1}^2 = \varepsilon_{i_3 j_3}^2$, and a downward jump at $\rho_{i_2 j_2}^2$.
```


<a id="sec:maximal"></a>
### From masked energies to the maximal transform

The estimate of Section <a href="#sec:jumps" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:jumps">5</a> allows different truncation endpoints at every output position. We first use this freedom on a finite mesh, choosing the endpoints that maximize each output. We then pass to functions and compare the resulting smooth kernels with hard truncations. The comparison will be pointwise and uniform in both endpoints.

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
#### Odd windows and selected endpoints

For $s>0$ and $0<\varepsilon<R$, define $$k_s(t)=\frac{t}{\sqrt{s}}g_s(t),\qquad
 \psi=k_1*k_1*k_1,\qquad
 I_{\varepsilon,R}(t)=\int_{\varepsilon^2}^{R^2}
       s^{-1/2}\psi(t/\sqrt{s})\,\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm ds}{s}.$$ Here $*$ is convolution on $\mathbb R$. The function $\psi$ is Schwartz, and $I_{\varepsilon,R}$ is smooth and integrable for fixed positive endpoints.

<a id="stmt-prop-selected-kernel"></a>
<div id="prop:selected-kernel" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:selected-kernel"></a>
**Proposition 14** (Selected smooth truncations). *Let $h>0$, let $N\ge0$ be an integer, set $u_i=ih$, and let $A_v(i,j)$, $v=0,1,2$, be complex arrays indexed by $-N\le i,j\le N$. Assign an arbitrary endpoint pair $h\le\varepsilon_{ij}<\rho_{ij}<\infty$ to each $(i,j)$. Then $$\label{eq:selected-kernel}
 \left|h^3\sum_{i,j,l}A_0(i,j)A_1(j,l)A_2(l,i)
       I_{\varepsilon_{ij},\rho_{ij}}(u_i+u_j+u_l)\right|
 \le C\prod_{v=0}^2\left(\sum_{i,j}h^2|A_v(i,j)|^3\right)^{1/3}.$$ The constant is independent of the arrays, mesh and selected endpoints.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-selected-kernel"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-selected-kernel</summary>

*Proof.* We apply the mixed trace inequality with Gaussian weights of larger variance than the windows $k_s$, so that the diagonal insertions are bounded. We then compare each quadratic cost with a variance choice covered by Proposition <a href="#prop:masked-dissipation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:masked-dissipation">13</a>.

Use the masked array $A_0^s(i,j)=A_0(i,j)\mathbf1_{\{\varepsilon_{ij}^2<s<\rho_{ij}^2\}}$ and the scale interval $[s_-,s_+]$ from Proposition <a href="#prop:masked-dissipation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:masked-dissipation">13</a>. All weighted matrices below use this same mask. Let superscript $\mathrm b$ denote the base variances $(\lambda s,\lambda s,\lambda s)$, where $\lambda=11/10$, and put $$D_v(i,i)=\frac{k_s(u_i-p_v)}{g_{\lambda s}(u_i-p_v)}.$$ These matrices are real diagonal. With $z=(u_i-p_v)/\sqrt{s}$, $$|D_v(i,i)|=\sqrt\lambda\,|z|
       e^{-(\lambda-1)z^2/(2\lambda)}
 \le\frac{\lambda}{\sqrt{e(\lambda-1)}}.$$ Thus their operator norms have an absolute bound.

<a id="eq:masked-window-integral"></a>
For each vertex $v$, let $\boldsymbol\alpha(v)$ equal $1$ at $v$ and $\lambda$ at the other two vertices. In the star centered at $v$, changing from the base variances to $\boldsymbol\alpha(v)s$ changes only its row weight. In particular, $$R_v^{\boldsymbol\alpha(v)}
 =\mathop{\mathrm{diag}}_i\left(\sqrt{\frac{g_s(u_i-p_v)}
                             {g_{\lambda s}(u_i-p_v)}}\right)R_v^{\mathrm b},
 \qquad
 T_v^{\boldsymbol\alpha(v)}\le\sqrt\lambda\,T_v^{\mathrm b}.$$ The last inequality uses $g_s\le\sqrt\lambda\,g_{\lambda s}$. The score $L_v^{\boldsymbol\alpha(v)}$ equals $\mathop{\mathrm{diag}}_i((u_i-p_v)/s)$, so the row densities also give the exact identity <a id="eq:maximal-window-score"></a>$$\label{eq:maximal-window-score}
 (R_v^{\mathrm b})^*D_vR_v^{\mathrm b}
 =\sqrt{s}\,(R_v^{\boldsymbol\alpha(v)})^*
           L_v^{\boldsymbol\alpha(v)}R_v^{\boldsymbol\alpha(v)}.$$ Indeed, both inserted row densities are $h k_s(u_i-p_v)$, and all column weights agree. The insertion on the left is therefore supported on $T_v^{\boldsymbol\alpha(v)}$. Lemma <a href="#lem:order" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:order">3</a> and quadratic homogeneity give $$b_{T_v^{\mathrm b}}\bigl((R_v^{\mathrm b})^*D_vR_v^{\mathrm b}\bigr)
 \le\lambda^{1/4}s V_v^{\boldsymbol\alpha(v)}.$$ Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:mixed-trace" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:mixed-trace">5</a> to the base matrices. Integrate its three nonnegative terms separately, using Proposition <a href="#prop:masked-dissipation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:masked-dissipation">13</a> with the corresponding $\boldsymbol\alpha(v)$. The factor $s$ cancels $\mathop{}\!\mathrm ds/s$, and we obtain $$\label{eq:masked-window-integral}
 \int_{s_-}^{s_+}\int_{\Pi_0}
 \left|\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(D_0W_0^{\mathrm b}D_1W_1^{\mathrm b}D_2W_2^{\mathrm b})\right|
       \,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\pi_0\,\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm ds}{s}
 \le C\mathcal S(A).$$

We identify the signed integral in this estimate. Expanding the trace leaves one signed Gaussian weight at each vertex: $$h^3\sum_{i,j,l}A_0^s(i,j)A_1(j,l)A_2(l,i)
       k_s(u_i-p_0)k_s(u_j-p_1)k_s(u_l-p_2).$$ On $\Pi_0$, substitute $r=u_i-p_0$ and $q=u_j-p_1$. The Jacobian is one and the third argument becomes $u_i+u_j+u_l-r-q$. Consequently the center integral is $(k_s*k_s*k_s)(u_i+u_j+u_l)$. Since $k_s(t)=s^{-1/2}k_1(t/\sqrt{s})$, this convolution equals $$s^{-1/2}\psi\bigl((u_i+u_j+u_l)/\sqrt{s}\bigr).$$ The sums are finite and the Gaussian products are absolutely integrable on the center plane and the compact positive scale interval. Thus all these integrations can be performed term by term. The mask restricts the scale integral of the $(i,j,l)$ term to $(\varepsilon_{ij}^2,\rho_{ij}^2)$. Taking the absolute value after integration in <a href="#eq:masked-window-integral" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:masked-window-integral">[eq:masked-window-integral]</a> proves <a href="#eq:selected-kernel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:selected-kernel">[eq:selected-kernel]</a> with its right side initially replaced by $C\mathcal S(A)$.

Put $n_v=(\sum h^2|A_v|^3)^{1/3}$. If any $n_v$ is zero the signed sum vanishes. Otherwise apply the last estimate to $A_v/n_v$ for all three vertices, keeping the selected endpoints fixed. The normalized value of $\mathcal S$ is $3$. Scaling the signed trilinear expression back proves the proposition. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-24"></a>
#### Finite maxima and the continuum limit

For functions $F,G$ on $\mathbb R^2$, write $$B^I_{\varepsilon,R}(F,G)(x,y)
   =\int_\mathbb RF(x+t,y)G(x,y+t)I_{\varepsilon,R}(t)\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm dt.$$ For compactly supported smooth inputs this integral is defined at every point. We now place the supremum over its endpoints inside the spatial norm.

<a id="stmt-prop-smooth-kernel-maximal"></a>
<div id="prop:smooth-kernel-maximal" class="proposition">

**Proposition 15**. *For all complex $F,G\in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R^2)$, $$\left\|\sup_{0<\varepsilon<R}
          |B^I_{\varepsilon,R}(F,G)|\right\|_{L^{3/2}(\mathbb R^2)}
 \le C\|F\|_{L^3(\mathbb R^2)}\|G\|_{L^3(\mathbb R^2)}.$$ The supremum is measurable and the constant is absolute.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-smooth-kernel-maximal"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-smooth-kernel-maximal</summary>

*Proof.* First fix a nonempty finite family of endpoint pairs $(\varepsilon_k,R_k)$, $1\le k\le m$, and take $h\le\min_k\varepsilon_k$. For finite arrays $A_1,A_2$, define $$U_k(i,j)=h\sum_l A_1(j,l)A_2(l,i)
                  I_{\varepsilon_k,R_k}(u_i+u_j+u_l),
 \qquad M(i,j)=\max_{1\le k\le m}|U_k(i,j)|.$$ At each output pair choose the least index attaining this maximum, and write $U(i,j)$ for its output. These choices provide admissible $\varepsilon_{ij},\rho_{ij}$ in Proposition <a href="#prop:selected-kernel" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:selected-kernel">14</a>. They are fixed while the testing array $A_0$ varies.

<a id="eq:discrete-finite-maximum"></a>
Set $H=\sum_{i,j}h^2 M(i,j)^{3/2}$. If $H=0$ the desired bound is immediate. Otherwise choose $$A_0(i,j)=
 \begin{cases}
 H^{-1/3}M(i,j)^{1/2}\,\overline{U(i,j)}/|U(i,j)|,
       &U(i,j)\ne0,\\
 0,&U(i,j)=0.
 \end{cases}$$ Then $\sum h^2|A_0|^3=1$ and $h^2\sum A_0U=H^{2/3}$. Proposition <a href="#prop:selected-kernel" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:selected-kernel">14</a> therefore gives $$\label{eq:discrete-finite-maximum}
 \left(\sum_{i,j}h^2M(i,j)^{3/2}\right)^{2/3}
 \le C\prod_{v=1}^2\left(\sum_{i,j}h^2|A_v(i,j)|^3\right)^{1/3}.$$ This estimate has no dependence on the number of endpoint pairs. The complex phase of $A_0$ accounts for the absolute value at each output.

To pass to functions, define the compactly supported smooth inputs $$G_1(b,c)=F(b+c,-b),\qquad G_2(c,a)=G(-a,c+a).$$ Both pair maps have determinant one, so $\|G_1\|_3=\|F\|_3$ and $\|G_2\|_3=\|G\|_3$. Choose $B>0$ so that both supports lie in the interior of $[-B,B]^2$. Take $h=B/N$, $-N\le i,j,l\le N$, and sample $$A_1(j,l)=G_1(jh,lh),\qquad A_2(l,i)=G_2(lh,ih).$$ The corresponding continuum outputs are $$\mathcal U_k(a,b)=\int_\mathbb RG_1(b,c)G_2(c,a)
                         I_{\varepsilon_k,R_k}(a+b+c)\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm dc.$$ They vanish outside $[-B,B]^2$: $G_2(c,a)$ restricts $a$ and $G_1(b,c)$ restricts $b$. A nonzero integrand also has $c\in[-B,B]$. For our fixed finite endpoint family the integrands and their $c$ derivatives are uniformly bounded on this compact cube. Riemann sums in $c$ consequently converge uniformly for $(a,b)\in[-B,B]^2$ and $1\le k\le m$. In particular, $$\max_{i,j}\max_{1\le k\le m}
       |U_k(i,j)-\mathcal U_k(ih,jh)|\longrightarrow0.$$ The finite maximum of magnitudes preserves this uniform convergence. Since $\max_k|\mathcal U_k|$ is continuous and compactly supported, the two-dimensional Riemann sums on the left of <a href="#eq:discrete-finite-maximum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:discrete-finite-maximum">[eq:discrete-finite-maximum]</a> converge to its $L^{3/2}$ norm. The cube roots of the input sums converge to $\|G_1\|_3$ and $\|G_2\|_3$. The condition on $h$ holds for all sufficiently large $N$. We obtain $$\|\max_k|\mathcal U_k|\|_{3/2}\le C\|G_1\|_3\|G_2\|_3.$$ No convergence or regularity of the maximizing indices is needed: we passed to the limit in the finite maximum itself.

With $x=-a$, $y=-b$, and $t=a+b+c$, the last integral is $B^I_{\varepsilon_k,R_k}(F,G)(x,y)$. The output coordinate change preserves Lebesgue measure, giving the asserted estimate for every finite endpoint family.

Finally enumerate all rational pairs $0<\varepsilon<R$ and take increasing finite subfamilies. At each spatial point the output is continuous in the two positive endpoints, by its defining integrals on compact scale and spatial intervals. Thus the supremum over these rational pairs equals the supremum over all real pairs. It is measurable as a countable supremum of continuous spatial functions. Monotone convergence of the $3/2$ powers of the finite maxima proves the result. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-25"></a>
#### A pointwise comparison with hard truncations

The smooth maximal estimate will imply the hard one because each endpoint error has a common pointwise majorant. A uniform bound on its kernel’s $L^1$ norm alone would not provide this comparison.

<a id="stmt-lem-maximal-hard-kernel"></a>
<div id="lem:maximal-hard-kernel" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:maximal-hard-decomposition"></a>
<a id="eq:maximal-error-envelope"></a>
**Lemma 16**. *There are an absolute constant $C$ and functions $E_r$, $r>0$, such that, for $t\ne0$, apart from endpoint values, $$\label{eq:maximal-hard-decomposition}
 I_{\varepsilon,R}(t)
  =\frac{c_0}{t}\mathbf1_{\{\varepsilon<|t|<R\}}
       +E_\varepsilon(t)-E_R(t),
 \qquad c_0=-\frac{2}{3\sqrt{6\pi}},$$ and $$\label{eq:maximal-error-envelope}
 |E_r(t)|\le\frac{C}{r}\left(1+\frac{|t|}{r}\right)^{-2}
 \qquad(t\ne0).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-maximal-hard-kernel"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-maximal-hard-kernel</summary>

*Proof.* Since $k_1=-g_1'$ and Gaussian variances add under convolution, $$\psi(t)=-g_3'''(t)
        =\left(\frac{t^3}{27}-\frac t3\right)g_3(t).$$ The convolution identity follows by completing the square; differentiation under convolution is justified by Schwartz decay. For $v\ge0$, put $$P(v)=2\int_0^v\psi(z)\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm dz
     =2g_3''(0)-2g_3''(v),\qquad
 c_0=P(\infty)=2g_3''(0)=-\frac2{3\sqrt{6\pi}}.$$ The substitution $z=t/\sqrt{s}$ for $t>0$, followed by oddness for $t<0$, yields $$I_{\varepsilon,R}(t)
   =\frac{P(|t|/\varepsilon)-P(|t|/R)}{t}.$$ Define $Q(v)=P(v)-c_0\mathbf1_{\{v>1\}}$ for $v\ge0$. Smooth oddness of $\psi$ gives $P(v)=O(v^2)$ at zero. For $v>1$, $$Q(v)=-2g_3''(v)
      =-2\left(\frac{v^2}{9}-\frac13\right)g_3(v).$$ Thus $Q$ has Gaussian decay at infinity and bounded one-sided values at its sole jump, $v=1$. Enlarging an absolute constant on a compact interval, we have $$\frac{|Q(v)|}{v}\le C(1+v)^{-2}\qquad(v>0).$$ Set $E_r(t)=Q(|t|/r)/t$ for $t\ne0$ and set $E_r(0)=0$. Substituting the definition of $Q$ proves <a href="#eq:maximal-hard-decomposition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:maximal-hard-decomposition">[eq:maximal-hard-decomposition]</a> and <a href="#eq:maximal-error-envelope" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:maximal-error-envelope">[eq:maximal-error-envelope]</a>. Values at the four hard endpoints have no effect on any integral. ◻

</details>

Let $M_x$ and $M_y$ denote the continuous centered interval maximal operators in the first and second variables, respectively. The next estimate follows from their one-dimensional bounds in Lemma <a href="#lem:line-maximal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:line-maximal">11</a>.

<a id="stmt-lem-pointwise-endpoint-error"></a>
<div id="lem:pointwise-endpoint-error" class="lemma">

**Lemma 17**. *For complex $F,G\in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R^2)$ and every $r>0$, $$\left|\int_\mathbb RF(x+t,y)G(x,y+t)E_r(t)\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm dt\right|
 \le C\bigl[M_x(|F|^2)(x,y)M_y(|G|^2)(x,y)\bigr]^{1/2}.$$ The right side has $L^{3/2}(\mathbb R^2)$ norm at most $C\|F\|_3\|G\|_3$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-pointwise-endpoint-error"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-pointwise-endpoint-error</summary>

*Proof.* Put $q_r(t)=r^{-1}(1+|t|/r)^{-2}$. For nonnegative locally integrable $f$ on $\mathbb R$, the part of $\int q_r(t)f(x+t)\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm dt$ with $|t|\le r$ is at most $2M_{\mathbb R}f(x)$. On the shell $2^kr<|t|\le2^{k+1}r$, $k\ge0$, its contribution is at most $$r^{-1}2^{-2k}\int_{|t|\le2^{k+1}r}f(x+t)\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm dt
 \le 4\,2^{-k}M_{\mathbb R}f(x).$$ Summing the geometric series proves a bound independent of $r$. By <a href="#eq:maximal-error-envelope" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:maximal-error-envelope">[eq:maximal-error-envelope]</a> and weighted Cauchy–Schwarz, $$\begin{aligned}
 \left|\int F(x+t,y)G(x,y+t)E_r(t)\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm dt\right|
 &\le C\left(\int |F(x+t,y)|^2q_r(t)\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm dt\right)^{1/2}\\
 &\hspace{12mm}\cdot
       \left(\int |G(x,y+t)|^2q_r(t)\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm dt\right)^{1/2}.
\end{aligned}$$ Apply the preceding one-dimensional bound on the respective coordinate lines. This gives the pointwise assertion.

Hölder’s inequality and Lemma <a href="#lem:line-maximal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:line-maximal">11</a>, applied on slices and then integrated in the other coordinate, give $$\begin{aligned}
 \|[M_x(|F|^2)M_y(|G|^2)]^{1/2}\|_{3/2}
 &\le\|M_x(|F|^2)\|_{3/2}^{1/2}
       \|M_y(|G|^2)\|_{3/2}^{1/2}\\
 &\le C\||F|^2\|_{3/2}^{1/2}\||G|^2\|_{3/2}^{1/2}
  =C\|F\|_3\|G\|_3.
\end{aligned}$$ ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-prop-compact-maximal"></a>
<div id="prop:compact-maximal" class="proposition">

**Proposition 18** (Compact smooth inputs). *For all complex $F,G\in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R^2)$, $$\|B_*(F,G)\|_{L^{3/2}(\mathbb R^2)}
 \le C\|F\|_{L^3(\mathbb R^2)}\|G\|_{L^3(\mathbb R^2)},$$ with an absolute constant. The supremum defining $B_*$ is over both finite hard truncation endpoints, inside the spatial norm.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-compact-maximal"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-compact-maximal</summary>

*Proof.* Lemma <a href="#lem:maximal-hard-kernel" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:maximal-hard-kernel">16</a> and Lemma <a href="#lem:pointwise-endpoint-error" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:pointwise-endpoint-error">17</a> give, at each spatial point, $$|c_0|\sup_{0<\varepsilon<R}|B_{\varepsilon,R}(F,G)|
 \le \sup_{0<\varepsilon<R}|B^I_{\varepsilon,R}(F,G)|
       +2C[M_x(|F|^2)M_y(|G|^2)]^{1/2}.$$ Both endpoint errors have the same majorant, independently of their radii. The hard outputs are continuous in both positive endpoints, so their supremum also equals the measurable supremum over rational pairs. Take $L^{3/2}$ norms, apply Proposition <a href="#prop:smooth-kernel-maximal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:smooth-kernel-maximal">15</a> and Lemma <a href="#lem:pointwise-endpoint-error" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:pointwise-endpoint-error">17</a>, and divide by $|c_0|>0$. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:consequences"></a>
### General inputs and principal values

We now pass the hard maximal estimate from compactly supported smooth inputs to all complex $L^3$ inputs. The main point is to control all truncations on one set of full measure. We then prove joint principal-value convergence and recover the scalar simplex formulation.

Write $p=3/2$ and $$B_{\varepsilon,R}(F,G)(x,y)
 =\int_{\varepsilon<|t|<R}F(x+t,y)G(x,y+t)\,\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm dt}{t}.$$ Proposition <a href="#prop:compact-maximal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:compact-maximal">18</a> supplies the hard maximal estimate for compactly supported smooth inputs; denote its constant by $C_M$.

<a id="stmt-lem-finite-truncations"></a>
<div id="lem:finite-truncations" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:elementary-bilinear"></a>
**Lemma 19** (Finite truncations). *For complex $F,G\in L^3(\mathbb R^2)$, there is a set of full measure on which every finite truncation is absolutely convergent and depends continuously on $(\varepsilon,R)$ in $0<\varepsilon<R<\infty$. The resulting family is independent of the representatives of $F,G$ up to a common null set. Moreover, $$\label{eq:elementary-bilinear}
 \left\|\int_{\varepsilon<|t|<R}
 |F(x+t,y)G(x,y+t)|\,\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm dt}{|t|}\right\|_p
 \le 2\log(R/\varepsilon)\|F\|_3\|G\|_3.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-finite-truncations"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-finite-truncations</summary>

*Proof.* For each fixed $t$, Hölder’s inequality and translation invariance give $\|F(\cdot+t,\cdot)G(\cdot,\cdot+t)\|_p\le\|F\|_3\|G\|_3$. Minkowski’s integral inequality proves <a href="#eq:elementary-bilinear" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:elementary-bilinear">[eq:elementary-bilinear]</a>. Apply it to the countable annuli $1/n<|t|<n$, $n\ge2$. Outside their combined exceptional null set, the integrand is absolutely integrable on every compact annulus away from zero. Absolute continuity of its integral gives the asserted endpoint continuity. If either input changes on a null set, apply the same bound to the zero $L^3$ difference, and use the same countable annuli. This proves independence simultaneously at every endpoint pair. ◻

</details>

Define $B_*(F,G)$ on this full-measure set as the supremum of $|B_{\varepsilon,R}(F,G)|$ over all finite endpoint pairs, and set it to zero elsewhere. Endpoint continuity makes this supremum equal to the one over positive rational endpoint pairs, so it is measurable.

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-finite-truncations-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-finite-truncations</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* Choose compactly supported smooth $f_m\to F$ and $g_m\to G$ in $L^3$. For a fixed endpoint pair, bilinearity and <a href="#eq:elementary-bilinear" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:elementary-bilinear">[eq:elementary-bilinear]</a> show that $B_{\varepsilon,R}(f_m,g_m)\to B_{\varepsilon,R}(F,G)$ in $L^p$. Fix a finite family of rational endpoint pairs. The difference of the two finite maxima of magnitudes is bounded by the sum of the corresponding truncation differences. Thus these maxima also converge in $L^p$. Passing the compact smooth maximal estimate to the limit bounds this finite maximum by $C_M\|F\|_3\|G\|_3$. The constant is independent of the family. Exhaust all rational endpoint pairs and use monotone convergence and Lemma <a href="#lem:finite-truncations" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:finite-truncations">19</a> to obtain Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.

We also obtain a useful continuity statement. For two pairs of inputs, bilinearity of every truncation gives, almost everywhere, <a id="eq:maximal-continuity"></a>$$\label{eq:maximal-continuity}
 |B_*(F,G)-B_*(f,g)|
 \le B_*(F-f,G)+B_*(f,G-g).$$ The bound just established makes the maximal map continuous from $L^3\times L^3$ to $L^{3/2}$. Density shows that it is the unique continuous extension of its compact smooth values. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-thm-bilinear-pv"></a>
<div id="thm:bilinear-pv" class="theorem">

<a id="eq:maximal-tail-convergence"></a>
**Theorem 20** (Joint bilinear principal values). *For all complex $F,G\in L^3(\mathbb R^2)$, the joint limit $$B(F,G)=\lim_{\substack{\varepsilon\downarrow0\\R\uparrow\infty}}
            B_{\varepsilon,R}(F,G)$$ exists almost everywhere and in $L^{3/2}(\mathbb R^2)$, with $\|B(F,G)\|_{3/2}\le C_M\|F\|_3\|G\|_3$. More precisely, $$\label{eq:maximal-tail-convergence}
 \left\|\sup_{\substack{0<\varepsilon<1/n\\R>n}}
 |B_{\varepsilon,R}(F,G)-B(F,G)|\right\|_{3/2}
 \longrightarrow0.$$ The limit is the unique bounded bilinear extension of the principal-value operator on compactly supported smooth inputs.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-thm-bilinear-pv"><summary>Proof of stmt-thm-bilinear-pv</summary>

*Proof.* For compactly supported smooth $f,g$ and a fixed $(x,y)$, the function $h(t)=f(x+t,y)g(x,y+t)$ is smooth and compactly supported. The truncated integral is $$\int_\varepsilon^R\frac{h(t)-h(-t)}t\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm dt.$$ Its numerator is $O(t)$ at zero and vanishes for large $t$, so it has a joint limit at every point.

For general inputs, let $$\Omega_n(F,G)=
 \sup_{\substack{0<\varepsilon,\varepsilon'<1/n\\R,R'>n}}
 |B_{\varepsilon,R}(F,G)-B_{\varepsilon',R'}(F,G)|.$$ These are measurable: by endpoint continuity the supremum may be taken over rational parameters. They decrease with $n$ and are bounded by $2B_*(F,G)$. Fix smooth approximants $f_m,g_m$ as above. Outside a single null set for this countable collection of input pairs, $$\Omega_n(F,G)\le\Omega_n(f_m,g_m)
       +2B_*(F-f_m,G)+2B_*(f_m,G-g_m).$$ Letting $n\to\infty$, the smooth joint convergence and Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> give $$\left\|\lim_{n\to\infty}\Omega_n(F,G)\right\|_p
 \le 2C_M\bigl(\|F-f_m\|_3\|G\|_3
              +\|f_m\|_3\|G-g_m\|_3\bigr).$$ The right side tends to zero with $m$. Hence the limiting oscillation is zero almost everywhere. This is the Cauchy criterion for all pairs of endpoints in the indicated tails, so the joint complex limit exists. It is measurable by taking a fixed cofinal sequence of endpoint pairs, and its magnitude is at most $B_*(F,G)$.

Taking $(\varepsilon',R')$ to the limit in the definition of $\Omega_n$ bounds the supremum in <a href="#eq:maximal-tail-convergence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:maximal-tail-convergence">[eq:maximal-tail-convergence]</a> by $\Omega_n(F,G)$. Dominated convergence applies because $0\le\Omega_n(F,G)\le2B_*(F,G)\in L^p$. This proves <a href="#eq:maximal-tail-convergence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:maximal-tail-convergence">[eq:maximal-tail-convergence]</a>, and hence joint norm convergence. Passing to the norm limit in the bilinear identities for the truncations proves bilinearity of $B$. Its bound and uniqueness follow from Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> and density. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-27"></a>
#### Scalar forms and the simplex coordinates

For complex $F_0,F_1,F_2\in L^3(\mathbb R^2)$, define the flat scalar form $$\mathcal L_{\varepsilon,R}(F_0,F_1,F_2)
 =\iiint_{\varepsilon<|t|<R}
 F_0(x,y)F_1(x+t,y)F_2(x,y+t)
 \,\mathop{}\!\mathrm dx\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm dy\,\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm dt}{t}.$$

<a id="stmt-cor-scalar-pv"></a>
<div id="cor:scalar-pv" class="corollary">

**Corollary 21** (Flat scalar principal values). *For complex $F_0,F_1,F_2\in L^3(\mathbb R^2)$, every finite flat truncation is absolutely integrable, and $$\sup_{0<\varepsilon<R<\infty}
 |\mathcal L_{\varepsilon,R}(F_0,F_1,F_2)|
 \le C_M\prod_{v=0}^2\|F_v\|_3.$$ Its joint scalar principal value exists and equals $\int F_0 B(F_1,F_2)$. This limiting form is the unique bounded trilinear extension of its compact smooth definition.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-scalar-pv"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-scalar-pv</summary>

*Proof.* Hölder’s inequality in $(x,y)$ bounds the absolute integral by $2\log(R/\varepsilon)\prod_v\|F_v\|_3$. Thus Fubini identifies the truncated form with $\int F_0 B_{\varepsilon,R}(F_1,F_2)$. Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> gives its endpoint-uniform bound, and Theorem <a href="#thm:bilinear-pv" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:bilinear-pv">20</a> gives joint scalar convergence by pairing the $L^{3/2}$ limit with $F_0$. The limiting form is bounded and complex trilinear. Density of compact smooth triples proves uniqueness. ◻

</details>

For the simplex form, set $$\Lambda_{\varepsilon,R}(G_0,G_1,G_2)
 =\iiint_{\varepsilon<|a+b+c|<R}
 G_0(a,b)G_1(b,c)G_2(c,a)
 \,\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm da\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm db\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm dc}{a+b+c}.$$

The flat and simplex scalar formulations, including equality of their optimal norm constants, are already related in (Kovač, Thiele, and Zorin-Kranich 2015, Appendix B.1) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[KovacThieleZorinKranich2015](#ref-KovacThieleZorinKranich2015)]. We record explicit norm-preserving maps and track both finite endpoints and their joint limit.

<a id="stmt-prop-flat-equivalence"></a>
<div id="prop:flat-equivalence" class="proposition">

**Proposition 22** (Scalar equivalence). *For all complex $G_0,G_1,G_2\in L^3(\mathbb R^2)$, every finite simplex truncation is absolutely integrable, and $$\sup_{0<\varepsilon<R<\infty}
 |\Lambda_{\varepsilon,R}(G_0,G_1,G_2)|
 \le C_M\prod_{v=0}^2\|G_v\|_3.$$ Its joint scalar principal value exists for every such triple and is the unique bounded trilinear extension of its compact smooth definition. The optimal constants in the uniform flat and simplex scalar estimates are equal.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-flat-equivalence"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-flat-equivalence</summary>

*Proof.* Use the determinant-one change of variables $$x=-a,\qquad y=-b,\qquad t=a+b+c,
 \qquad
 a=-x,\quad b=-y,\quad c=t+x+y.$$ For flat inputs define $$\begin{aligned}
 G_0(u,v)&=F_0(-u,-v),\\
 G_1(u,v)&=F_1(u+v,-u),\\
 G_2(u,v)&=F_2(-v,u+v).
 \end{aligned}$$ Each pair map has determinant one. Their inverses are $$F_0(r,s)=G_0(-r,-s),\quad
 F_1(r,s)=G_1(-s,r+s),\quad
 F_2(r,s)=G_2(r+s,-r).$$ They preserve $L^3$ norms, compact smoothness, and Schwartz class. Direct substitution identifies the flat and simplex integrands and their truncation regions. The absolute integrability, uniform bound, and joint scalar convergence therefore follow from the flat conclusions. The inverse maps realize every simplex triple, which proves equality of the two optimal scalar constants. Density gives uniqueness of the bounded trilinear extension. ◻

</details>

Write $C_S$ for the common optimal scalar constant. Complex $L^p$ duality shows that it is also the optimal constant for the uniform family of individual bounds $$\|B_{\varepsilon,R}(F,G)\|_{3/2}
 \le C_S\|F\|_3\|G\|_3.$$ Indeed, for nonzero $U\in L^p$, the function $$H=\frac{\overline U}{|U|}\,
       \frac{|U|^{1/2}}{\|U\|_p^{1/2}},$$ set to zero where $U=0$, has $L^3$ norm one and satisfies $\int HU=\|U\|_p$. Apply this to each bilinear truncation; the zero case is immediate. The pointwise maximal optimal constant is at least $C_S$.

```json
  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "The three edge maps and the row star at vertex $0$. Taking the adjoint of $W_{2}$ makes both blocks of $R_{0}$ have target $H_{0}$. The two Grams $R_{0}^{*}R_{0}$ and $R_{0}R_{0}^{*}$ have the same positive eigenvalues on different spaces.",
      "figure_id": "fig:triangle",
      "pdf_page": 6,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:triangle",
          "exact_quote": "The three edge maps and the row star at vertex $0$.\nTaking the adjoint of $W_2$ makes both blocks of $R_0$\nhave target $H_0$. The two Grams $R_0^*R_0$ and $R_0R_0^*$\nhave the same positive eigenvalues on different spaces.",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[>=Stealth, every node/.style={font=\\small},\n space/.style={circle,draw=black!60,minimum size=9mm,inner sep=2pt},\n edge/.style={->,thick,blue!65!black}]\n \\node[space] (h0) at (0,1.9) {$H_0$};\n \\node[space] (h1) at (-1.8,-.8) {$H_1$};\n \\node[space] (h2) at (1.8,-.8) {$H_2$};\n \\draw[edge] (h1) -- node[left=5pt] {$W_0$} (h0);\n \\draw[edge] (h2) -- node[below=5pt] {$W_1$} (h1);\n \\draw[edge] (h0) -- node[right=5pt] {$W_2$} (h2);\n \\node[below=18pt] at (0,-.8) {the cyclic product $W_0W_1W_2$};\n \\node[space] (r1) at (5,1.3) {$H_1$};\n \\node[space] (r2) at (5,-.8) {$H_2$};\n \\node[space] (r0) at (7.5,.25) {$H_0$};\n \\draw[edge] (r1) -- node[above=5pt] {$W_0$} (r0);\n \\draw[edge] (r2) -- node[below=5pt] {$W_2^*$} (r0);\n \\node[below=18pt] at (6.25,-.8) {$R_0=[\\,W_0\\quad W_2^*\\,]$};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "Two diagrams side by side:\n- Left: A directed triangle with vertices $H_0$ (top), $H_1$ (bottom-left), and $H_2$ (bottom-right). Blue directed edges: $W_0$ pointing from $H_1$ to $H_0$, $W_1$ pointing from $H_2$ to $H_1$, and $W_2$ pointing from $H_0$ to $H_2$. Subtitle label: \"the cyclic product $W_0W_1W_2$\".\n- Right: A graph with vertices $H_1$ (top-left), $H_2$ (bottom-left), and $H_0$ (right). Blue directed edges: $W_0$ pointing from $H_1$ to $H_0$, and $W_2^*$ pointing from $H_2$ to $H_0$. Subtitle label: \"$R_0 = [ W_0 \\quad W_2^* ]$\"."
    },
    {
      "caption": "Three selected output pairs and a schematic integrated energy, not an exact heat-flow solution. The entry $A_{0}^{s}(i,j)$ is retained only on $(ε_{ij}^{2},ρ_{ij}^{2})$. Between successive event scales, $J(s)$ is nonincreasing by the fixed-array dissipation inequality. Dashed arrows connect its one-sided limits at events; the values at the events themselves are not shown. Jumps may have either sign, and endpoints may coincide, as illustrated by $ρ_{i1}j_{1}^{2} = ε_{i3}j_{3}^{2}$. The bound $∑_{τ}|ΔJ(τ)| ≤ C𝒮(A)$, together with the initial-energy bound, controls the accumulated dissipation independently of the number of event scales.",
      "figure_id": "fig:mask-energy",
      "pdf_page": 18,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:mask-energy",
          "exact_quote": "Three selected output pairs and a schematic integrated energy,\nnot an exact heat-flow solution. The entry $A_0^s(i,j)$ is retained only\non $(\\varepsilon_{ij}^2,\\rho_{ij}^2)$. Between successive event scales,\n$J(s)$ is nonincreasing by the f",
          "source_object": "fig:0002"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[x=1cm,y=1cm,font=\\small,\n  active/.style={line width=2pt,draw=blue!65!black},\n  event/.style={densely dotted,draw=black!24},\n  endpoint/.style={circle,draw=blue!65!black,fill=white,\n    line width=.65pt,inner sep=0pt,minimum size=4pt},\n  endpoint label/.style={font=\\footnotesize,fill=white,inner sep=2pt},\n  energy/.style={line width=1pt,draw=black},\n  jump/.style={-{Stealth[length=3pt]},densely dashed,\n    line width=.8pt,draw=red!65!black}]\n  \\foreach \\t in {2.3,3.8,6.4,9.0,11.6}\n    \\draw[event] (\\t,.1)--(\\t,6.15);\n\n  \\node[anchor=west] at (1.9,6.65) {Output-dependent scale intervals};\n  \\foreach \\yy in {5.8,4.8,3.8}\n    \\draw[black!22] (1.95,\\yy)--(12.25,\\yy);\n  \\node[anchor=east] at (1.7,5.8) {$(i_1,j_1)$};\n  \\node[anchor=east] at (1.7,4.8) {$(i_2,j_2)$};\n  \\node[anchor=east] at (1.7,3.8) {$(i_3,j_3)$};\n  \\draw[active] (2.3,5.8)--(6.4,5.8);\n  \\draw[active] (3.8,4.8)--(9.0,4.8);\n  \\draw[active] (6.4,3.8)--(11.6,3.8);\n  \\foreach \\xx/\\yy in {2.3/5.8,6.4/5.8,3.8/4.8,9.0/4.8,6.4/3.8,11.6/3.8}\n    \\node[endpoint] at (\\xx,\\yy) {};\n  \\node[endpoint label,above] at (2.3,5.91) {$\\varepsilon_{i_1j_1}^{\\,2}$};\n  \\node[endpoint label,above] at (6.4,5.91) {$\\rho_{i_1j_1}^{\\,2}$};\n  \\node[endpoint label,above] at (3.8,4.91) {$\\varepsilon_{i_2j_2}^{\\,2}$};\n  \\node[endpoint label,above] at (9.0,4.91) {$\\rho_{i_2j_2}^{\\,2}$};\n  \\node[endpoint label,above] at (6.4,3.91) {$\\varepsilon_{i_3j_3}^{\\,2}$};\n  \\node[endpoint label,above] at (11.6,3.91) {$\\rho_{i_3j_3}^{\\,2}$};\n  \\draw[-{Stealth[length=4pt]},black!65] (1.95,3.35)--(12.6,3.35)\n    node[right] {$s$};\n\n  \\node[anchor=west,fill=white,inner sep=2pt] at (1.9,2.9) {Integrated energy};\n  \\draw[-{Stealth[length=4pt]},black!65] (1.95,.1)--(12.6,.1)\n    node[right] {$s$};\n  \\node[anchor=east] at (1.7,1.7) {$J(s)$};\n  \\draw[energy] (2.3,2.2) .. controls (2.9,2.05) and (3.3,1.91) .. (3.8,1.82);\n  \\draw[jump] (3.8,1.82)--(3.8,2.38);\n  \\draw[energy] (3.8,2.38) .. controls (4.7,2.13) and (5.4,1.85) .. (6.4,1.64);\n  \\draw[jump] (6.4,1.64)--(6.4,1.97);\n  \\draw[energy] (6.4,1.97) .. controls (7.2,1.75) and (8.1,1.37) .. (9.0,1.18);\n  \\draw[jump] (9.0,1.18)--(9.0,.78);\n  \\draw[energy] (9.0,.78) .. controls (9.9,.61) and (10.7,.43) .. (11.6,.32);\n  \\node[below] at (2.3,.02) {$s_-$};\n  \\node[below] at (11.6,.02) {$s_+$};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "Two vertically aligned diagrams:\n- Top diagram (\"Output-dependent scale intervals\"): Horizontal axis labeled $s$ with an arrow pointing right. Three rows labeled $(i_1, j_1)$, $(i_2, j_2)$, and $(i_3, j_3)$ showing intervals drawn in blue, each with an open circle at the left endpoint and a filled circle at the right endpoint. Endpoints are marked $\\varepsilon_{i_1 j_1}^2$ and $\\rho_{i_1 j_1}^2$ for the first pair; $\\varepsilon_{i_2 j_2}^2$ and $\\rho_{i_2 j_2}^2$ for the second pair; $\\varepsilon_{i_3 j_3}^2$ and $\\rho_{i_3 j_3}^2$ for the third pair. The point $\\rho_{i_1 j_1}^2$ shares the same horizontal position as $\\varepsilon_{i_3 j_3}^2$. Light vertical guide lines extend downward from all endpoints.\n- Bottom diagram (\"Integrated energy\"): Horizontal axis labeled $s$ with an arrow pointing right, marked with endpoints $s_-$ and $s_+$. Vertical axis labeled $J(s)$. The function $J(s)$ is plotted as solid black curves that decrease within each interval between events. At event coordinates corresponding to interval endpoints above, red dashed vertical arrows connect one-sided limits: an upward jump at $\\varepsilon_{i_2 j_2}^2$, a downward jump at $\\rho_{i_1 j_1}^2 = \\varepsilon_{i_3 j_3}^2$, and a downward jump at $\\rho_{i_2 j_2}^2$."
    }
  ],
```
